Ultra Kolyvagin systems and higher Fitting ideals of Iwasawa Selmer groups
Alberto Angurel

Abstract
We develop the theory of equivariant, ultra Kolyvagin systems to bypass structural limitations of the Euler system machinery over infinite rings. By utilizing collections of classes living in the exterior powers of patched Selmer groups—constructed from ultraproducts of classical Selmer groups—we compute the structure of an Iwasawa Selmer group up to pseudo-isomorphism of Iwasawa modules and prove the absence of finite submodules. We apply this theoretical framework to the fine Selmer group of an elliptic curve and the Bloch-Kato Selmer group of the Rankin-Selberg convolution of modular forms.



1
Introduction

The method of Euler systems is a cornerstone of Iwasawa theory that has led to considerable progress towards the proof of important conjectures in arithmetic geometry. Arguably, its most important consequence is the proof of the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture for elliptic curves whose analytic rank is at most one. Furthermore, this method has also been applied in greater generality to prove the Bloch-Kato conjecture and one divisibility of the Iwasawa main conjecture in other settings.

Historically, this argument was introduced in
[
33
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
10
]
and
[
29
]
, where Selmer groups were studied using collections of cohomology classes, known as Euler systems, satisfying certain norm-compatibility relations with a similar flavour to the Euler factors used to define the
L
-function. After that, Mazur and Rubin axiomatized in
[
21
]
the ideas at the core of this argument by introducing the notion of Kolyvagin systems.

In this article, we generalise the theory of Kolyvagin systems to prove refinements of the Iwasawa main conjectures. In addition to the equality between the
p
-adic
L
-function and the characteristic ideal of the dual Selmer group predicted by this conjecture, we are able to describe its full structure up to pseudo-isomorphism of Iwasawa modules in terms of higher Stickelberger ideals, similar to those appearing in
[
16
]
. Although our methods can be applied in wider generality, we give explicit computations for the Selmer groups associated with elliptic curves and Rankin-Selberg convolutions of modular forms.

Our work builds on further generalisations of the theory of Kolyvagin systems in
[
22
]
,
[
5
]
,
[
30
]
,
[
4
]
and
[
3
]
. They extended the results in
[
21
]
in two different directions: Selmer groups with higher core rank and those with non-principal coefficient rings, allowing an equivariant generalisation of the theory.

Here we develop a further generalisation of the theory of Kolyvagin systems that gives a full description of the Selmer group at the top of a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension. In order to do this, we develop an equivariant theory of Kolyvagin systems of higher rank that combines previous methods in
[
4
]
and
[
32
]
.

We view Selmer groups as subgroups of the patched cohomology, originally constructed in
[
32
]
as an ultraproduct of classical Galois cohomology groups. In this setting, the notion of prime number is relaxed, leading to the concept of ultraprimes. The existence of ultraprimes satisfying properties not satisfied by any prime is what allows this generalisation of the theory of Kolyvagin systems. This new formalism was used by Loeffler and Zerbes in
[
19
]
to generalise the Euler system argument for bounding Selmer groups associated with non-ordinary Galois representations.

This setting allows us to develop the Kolyvagin system argument directly on the profinite Selmer groups that naturally appear when studying the Galois representation along a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension. It is a significant advantage over the previous theory, which requires one to go through the cohomology of the finite quotients. In our new formalism, the full power of the structure theorem of finitely generated Iwasawa modules can be used to describe the structure of the dual Selmer group.

In particular, we develop a higher rank theory of ultra Kolyvagin systems, which are seen as collections of classes in the exterior powers of patched Selmer groups. In this formalism, the Stickelberger ideals associated with a primitive Kolyvagin system are more than bounds of the Selmer group, but can describe its pseudo-isomorphism structure. In addition, the existence of ultra Kolyvagin systems shows, under mild big image assumptions, the absence of finite submodules in Iwasawa Selmer groups.

Using the methods presented in this article, we are able to compute the structure of the fine Selmer group of an elliptic curve and the Bloch-Kato Selmer group of the Rankin-Selberg convolution of modular forms along the cyclotomic
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension. However, the method can be applied to a wider class of Galois representations for which Euler systems are known.

1.1
Main Results

We propose a generalisation of the theory of Kolyvagin systems which allows the computation of the higher Fitting ideals of a Selmer group associated with a Galois representation over the Iwasawa algebra

\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[X]],

where
p\geq 5
is a prime number. We assume that
T
is a free, finitely generated
\Lambda
-module endowed with a continuous action of the absolute Galois group of a number field
K
that is ramified only at finitely many primes.

We will consider the Selmer group
H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
defined by a cartesian Selmer structure of positive core rank . In order to develop the theory, we need to impose certain mild big image assumptions and the
p
-primality of the Tamagawa numbers. Precise definitions and assumptions are detailed in §
4
-
5
.

We establish the notion of ultra Kolyvagin systems of higher rank and we show, under the assumptions previously mentioned, that the Iwasawa module of ultra Kolyvagin systems
\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
is free of rank one. Hence we can fix a generator
\kappa
of
\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
, which is referred to as a primitive Kolyvagin system.

In addition, when
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
is a Kolyvagin system, we can define a sequence of ideals
\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)
, where
i\geq 0
, from the divisibility of the different classes in
\kappa
(see Definition
8.4
).

This sequence of ideals can be used to describe the structure of the dual Selmer group. By local duality, it is possible to define a dual Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}^{*}
, that will determine a Selmer group on the cohomology of the Cartier dual
\textbf{T}^{*}
. It is the structure of its Pontryagin dual
H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
that can be determined using the ideals
\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)
.

Theorem 1.1
.

(Theorem
8.25
)
Let
T
and
\mathcal{F}
be as above and let
\kappa
be a primitive Kolyvagin system. Then the ideals
\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)
coincide, up to finite index, with the Fitting ideals
\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)
of the dual Selmer group.

Theorem
1.1
gives enough information about the Fitting ideals to compute the Iwasawa Selmer module up to pseudo-isomorphism. However, it can be refined in order to prove the absence of finite submodules in the Selmer group.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Theorem
9.15
)
Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, suppose that
\mathcal{F}
also satisfies Assumption
(Prop)
from section §
9.2
. Then

\Theta_{i}(\kappa)=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\ \ \forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

This finer computation of Fitting ideals can be used to show that the Pontryagin duals of Iwasawa Selmer groups contain no finite submodules or, equivalently, that they have projective dimension one. The next result is a generalisation of previous theorems in
[
9
, Propositions 4.8 and 4.9]
, concerning the Selmer group of an elliptic curve, and
[
16
, Proposition 2.9]
, which assumed stronger assumptions such as the triviality of the
\mu
-invariant associated with elliptic curves.

Corollary 1.3
.

(Theorem
9.16
)
In the setting of Theorem
1.2
, if
H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
is a torsion Iwasawa module, then it contains no finite submodules.

These results, particularly Theorem
1.2
, can be applied to compute the higher Fitting ideal of the Iwasawa Selmer groups of some specific representations for which an Euler system is known. Particularly, we apply our theory to the following two situations:

Theorem 1.4
.

(Theorem
10.1
)
Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be an elliptic curve defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and let
T_{p}E
be its
p
-adic Tate module. Let
\kappa_{\textrm{Kato}}
be the Kolyvagin systems obtained from Kato’s Euler system
[
11
]
. Under Assumptions
•
‣
10.1
-
•
‣
10.1
in §
10.1
,

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa_{\textrm{Kato}})=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\Sel_{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty},E[p^{\infty}])^{\vee}\Bigr)\ \ \forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

where
\Sel_{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty},E[p^{\infty}])
denotes the fine Selmer group, with restricted local condition at
p
.

Theorem 1.5
.

(Theorem
10.9
)
Let
T
be the Galois representation associated with the Rankin-Selberg convolution of two ordinary modular forms which satisfies Assumptions
•
‣
(RS)
-
•
‣
(RS)
in §
10.2
and the Iwasawa main conjecture. Let
\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}}
be the Kolyvagin system obtained from the Euler system of Lei-Loeffler-Zerbes
[
17
]
. Then

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}})=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(H^{1}_{{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty},T^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\ \ \forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

However, our methods can be applied in greater generality to Galois representations for which the existence of Euler systems is known.

1.2
Relation with the existing literature

The existing literature on the theory of Kolyvagin systems was limited to the computation of the characteristic ideal of the dual Selmer group using the leading term of the system. Here we generalise this theory to compute its full structure.

This article is a natural generalisation of
[
4
]
. In loc. cit., a sequence of ideals
\Theta_{i}(\kappa)
was used to establish bounds on the Fitting ideals of the Selmer group and it is conjectured that these bounds are sharp.

Here we can give an affirmative answer to this conjecture for representations with coefficients over the Iwasawa algebra by developing the theory in the patched cohomology groups constructed in
[
32
]
. It allows a more powerful use of the structure theorem of Iwasawa modules that leads to the full computation of these Selmer groups.

Patched cohomology has been previously used in
[
19
]
for developing the Euler system argument in order to bound Selmer groups that were out of the scope of the original Euler system argument. In loc. cit., a patched version of the Kolyvagin derivative is developed so one can obtain, from an Euler system, ultra Kolyvagin systems satisfying appropriate local conditions in non-ordinary settings. This generalisation allowed the application of the Euler system machinery to new Galois representations, establishing explicit bounds to their associated Selmer groups.

The generalisation made in this article also allows a simplification of the method in
[
4
]
, since Kolyvagin systems can be constructed as collections of classes in the exterior powers of Iwasawa Selmer groups. The reason is that, under our assumptions, the profinite Selmer groups are free Iwasawa modules, so there is a canonical identification between their exterior powers and their exterior biduals, the objects used in
[
4
]
to construct higher rank Kolyvagin systems.

Our main results here have been applied to determine the structure of the Selmer groups associated with an elliptic curve and with the Rankin-Selberg convolution of two ordinary modular forms. The method used to obtain ultra Kolyvagin systems was to compare them with the limit of classical Kolyvagin systems, which can be obtained from an Euler system using the standard argument in
[
21
]
. However, this comparison with classical Kolyvagin systems is not needed, since ultra Kolyvagin systems can be directly obtained from Euler systems using the patched version of the Kolyvagin derivative developed in
[
19
]
.

Previous computations of higher Fitting ideals of Iwasawa Selmer groups in the literature were limited to particular cases or required much stronger assumptions. The first results in this direction appear in
[
15
]
,
[
27
]
and
[
25
]
, concerning the limit of class groups along a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension. Those works were generalised in
[
16
]
and
[
26
]
, computing the higher Fitting ideals of Selmer groups defined for a Galois representation that is not residually self-dual. Our results can be seen as a generalisation of these results, since they do not require those assumptions about self-duality.

Specially interesting is the recent preprint
[
6
]
, which studies the higher Fitting ideals for the self-dual representation associated with the Tate module of an elliptic curve in the anticyclotomic setting. This Selmer group is out of the scope of the methods of this article, since it is defined from a Selmer structure of core rank
0
. It remains an interesting question to extend the theory of ultra Kolyvagin systems of Iwasawa modules to obtain information about these Selmer groups, generalising the results in
[
1
]
in this equivariant setting.

1.3
Structure

The structure of this article is the following. §
2
-§
4
are dedicated to outlining the construction of the patched cohomology from
[
32
]
, as well as including the technical results needed for the development of this theory.

In §
5
, we specialize the (patched) Selmer groups obtained from Galois representations in which the coefficient ring is the Iwasawa algebra. The algebraic preliminaries, including the construction of the Fitting ideals and the rank reduction maps of exterior powers are included in §
6
.

§
7
and §
8
are dedicated to the construction of ultra Stark and ultra Kolyvagin systems, respectively, whose classes live in the exterior powers of patched Selmer groups. §
8
includes the first main result in this article, Theorem
8.25
, that describes the Fitting ideals of the Selmer group up to finite index.

§
9
compares the newly constructed Kolyvagin system with the classical theory of Kolyvagin systems in
[
4
]
. This comparison allows a finer description of the Fitting ideal (see Theorem
9.15
), which are now completely determined by the ideals
\Theta_{i}(\kappa)
.

Finally, an application of this theorem to the ultra Kolyvagin systems obtained from the Euler systems constructed by Kato
[
11
]
and Lei-Loeffler-Zerbes
[
17
]
appears in §
10
.

Acknowledgements

The author would like to thank Christian Wuthrich for numerous discussions during the preparation of this manuscript. The author is also grateful to Dominik Bullach and David Loeffler for enlightening conversations about the topics in this manuscript.

2
Ultrafilters

2.1
Ultraproducts

Definition 2.1
.

A
filter
in the natural numbers is a collection of sets
\mathcal{U}
in the power set
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})
such that

•

(F0) The empty set does not belong to
\mathcal{U}
.

•

(F1) If
S_{1}\subset S_{2}
and
S_{1}\in\mathcal{U}
, then
S_{2}\in\mathcal{U}
.

•

(F2) If
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathcal{U}
, then
S_{1}\cap S_{2}\in\mathcal{U}
.

We say that a filter is an
ultrafilter
if it also satisfies the following condition

•

(UF) For every set
S\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})
, either
S\in\mathcal{U}
or
\mathbb{N}\setminus S\in\mathcal{U}
.

The key property of ultrafilters is that
•
‣
2.1
generalises to finite partitions, i.e., ultrafilters contain exactly one set in every finite partition of
\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 2.2
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.1.2]
)
Let
\mathcal{U}
be an ultrafilter, let
S\in\mathcal{U}
and let
C
be a finite set. For every map
f:\ S\to C
, there exists a unique
c\in C
such that
f^{-1}(c)\in\mathcal{U}
.

Notation 2.3
.

When we say that some property is satisfied for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we mean that there is a set
S\in\mathcal{U}
such that the property holds for every
i\in S
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{N}
, the set

\mathcal{U}_{a}=\{S\subset\mathbb{N}:\ a\in S\}

is an ultrafilter. We say that an ultrafilter is
principal
if there is an element
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{a}
.

For this theory, interesting ultrafilters are non-principal ones. They cannot be constructed explicitly, but their existence is guaranteed by the axiom of choice. Note that every non-principal ultrafilter contains the Fréchet filter, formed by the subset with finite complement.

Ultrafilters will be used to define the ultraproduct, which is a functor that cluster together infinitely many groups.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an ultrafilter and let
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of sets. The
ultraproduct

\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n})
is the set defined as

\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n})=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{n}\biggm/\sim,

where the product is the direct product of sets and
\sim
is the equivalence relation defined as
(m_{n})\sim(m^{\prime}_{n})
if
m_{n}=m^{\prime}_{n}
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
n
.

Notation 2.5
.

We will also denote the ultraproduct by
\mathcal{U}(M_{n})
when there is no risk of confusion. In addition, for every set
M
, we will denote by
\mathcal{U}(M)
the ultraproduct of the sequence
(M_{n})
in which
M_{n}=M
for all
n
. The latter will be also called the
ultrapower
of
M
.

Remark 2.6
.

If the sets
M_{n}
are groups (resp. rings, modules, etc.), the ultraproduct
\mathcal{U}(M_{n})
has a natural group structure (resp. ring structure, module structure, etc.).

Proposition 2.7
.

(
[
32
, Propositions 2.1.4 and 2.1.5]
)
The ultraproduct
\mathcal{U}
is functorial. In addition, it is exact when seen in the category of pointed sets.

In general, it is difficult to compute the ultraproduct, but there is a special case in which it is explicit: the ultraproduct of a sequence of finite sets of bounded order.

Lemma 2.8
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.1.4. (ii)]
)
If
M
is a finite set, the diagonal map
\Delta:\ M\to\mathcal{U}(M)
is an isomorphism.

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
R
be a finite ring and let
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
R
-modules of bounded order. Then
\mathcal{U}(M_{n})\cong M_{n}
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
n
.

Proof.

Let
C
be a bound on
\#M_{n}
. Since
R
is finite, there are only finitely many isomorphism classes of
R
-modules with order smaller than
C
. Then Proposition
2.2
implies that exactly one of these isomorphism classes contains
\mathcal{U}
-many
M_{n}
. By Lemma
2.8
, this class also contains
\mathcal{U}(M_{n})
.
∎

Now we state some properties of the ultraproduct.

Remark 2.10
.

If
(A_{n})
and
(B_{n})
are sequences of sets, modules or rings, there is a canonical identification

\mathcal{U}(A_{n}\times B_{n})=\mathcal{U}(A_{n})\times\mathcal{U}(B_{n}),

which is well defined because the ultrafilter satisfies
•
‣
2.1
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be two sequences in
R
-modules. Then there is an injection

\Psi:\ \mathcal{U}(\Hom_{R}(A_{n},B_{n}))\hookrightarrow\Hom_{R}\Bigl(\mathcal{U}(A_{n}),\mathcal{U}(B_{n})\Bigr).

In addition,
\Psi
is an isomorphism when
(B_{n})
is a constant sequence of finite sets.

Proof.

Let
(\varphi_{n})
be the sequence representing an element in the ultraproduct
\mathcal{U}(\Hom_{R}(A_{n},B_{n}))
and let
(a_{n})
be a sequence representing an element in
\mathcal{U}(A_{n})
. We assign the sequence
\varphi_{n}(a_{n})\in\mathcal{U}(B_{n})
. Clearly, this assignment behaves well under the ultrafilter equivalences in both
\mathcal{U}(\Hom(A_{n},B_{n}))
and
\mathcal{U}(A_{n})
, so it defines a map

\Psi:\mathcal{U}(\Hom(A_{n},B_{n}))\to\Hom\Bigl(\mathcal{U}(A_{n}),\mathcal{U}(B_{n})\Bigr).

In order to check injectivity, consider another sequence
(\psi_{n})
induces the same element in
\Hom\Bigl(\mathcal{U}(A_{n}),\mathcal{U}(B_{n})\Bigr)
. For the sake of contradiction, assume that
(\varphi_{n})
and
(\psi_{n})
are not equivalent, i.e., the set

S=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\ \varphi_{n}\neq\psi_{n}\}\in\mathcal{U}.

Choose a sequence
(a_{n})\in(A_{n})
where
\varphi_{n}(a_{n})\neq\psi_{n}(a_{n})
for every
n\in S
. Hence
(\varphi_{n}(a_{n}))
and
(\psi_{n}(a_{n}))
represent different elements in
\mathcal{U}(B_{n})
, so
\Psi(\varphi_{n})\neq\Psi(\psi_{n})
.

Assume that
B_{n}=B
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
B
is a finite group. Then every element in
\Hom\Bigl(\mathcal{U}(A_{n}),B\Bigr)
lifts to a homomorphism in

\Hom\left(\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_{n},B\right)=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\Hom(A_{n},B)\right),

so it is also in the image of
\Psi
.
∎

2.2
Ultraprimes

For the rest of this paper, fix a non-principal ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
and a number field
K
.

Notation 2.12
.

Denote by
\mathcal{M}_{K}
the set of places of
K
.

Definition 2.13
.

An
ultraprime

\mathfrak{u}
is an element of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
. More specifically, it is represented by a sequence of prime ideals
(\ell_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and two sequences represent the same ultraprime if they coincide in
\mathcal{U}
-many primes.

Remark 2.14
.

The primes in
\mathcal{M}_{K}
are contained in the ultraprimes
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
via the diagonal map, i.e., a prime
\ell
is identified with the equivalence class of the constant sequence
(\ell)
. The elements in the image of the map
\mathcal{M}_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
are sometimes referred as
constant ultraprimes
.

We can use Proposition
2.2
to define the Frobenius element associated with an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}
in the absolute Galois group
G_{K}
.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{n})
be an ultraprime and let
L/K
be a finite Galois extension of number fields. Then there exists a unique conjugacy class in
\Gal(L/K)
such that
\textrm{Frob}_{\ell_{n}}|_{L/K}\in c
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
n
. This class is called the
Frobenius conjugacy class
of
\mathfrak{u}
at
L/K
and any element in it is called a
Frobenius automorphism

\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{n})
be an ultraprime. The (absolute) Frobenius conjugacy class
\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is defined as the conjugacy class

\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\left(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}|_{L/K}\right)_{L/K}\subset\varprojlim_{L/K}\Gal(L/K)=G_{K}.

Any automorphism in this class is called a Frobenius element
\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Proposition 2.17
.

(
[
19
, Lemma 5.4]
)
Let
K
be a number field and let
c\subset G_{K}
be a conjugacy class. Then there exists an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}
such that
\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=c
.

The construction of the Frobenius is used to artificially define the local Galois group at the ultraprime. It is a generalization of the tame quotient of the classical local Galois groups. This construction was originally done in
[
32
]
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}
be an ultraprime represented by a sequence
(\ell_{i})_{i\in N}
. The local Galois group
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
is defined as the profinite completion of
\mathcal{U}(G_{\ell_{i}})
. Similarly, the inertia subgroup
I_{\mathfrak{u}}
is obtained similarly as the profinite completion of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{\ell_{i}})
.

Remark 2.19
.

When
\mathfrak{u}
is a non-constant ultraprime, the
local Galois group

G_{\mathfrak{u}}
is defined as the semidirect product

G_{\mathfrak{u}}:=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)\rtimes\langle\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}\rangle,

where
\langle\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}\rangle
is the free profinite group generated by one element which acts by
\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}\in G_{K}
on
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
. Note that this action is well-defined since
K(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/K
is an abelian exension.

The
inertia subgroup

\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}\subset G_{\mathfrak{u}}
is identified with the normal subgroup
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)\subset G_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Remark 2.20
.

When
\mathfrak{u}
is a constant ultraprime, the semidirect product in Remark
2.19
represents the tame inertia quotient of the Galois group
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
.

Remark 2.21
.

Assume
T
is a finite
G_{\ell}
-module for every prime
\ell
. By Proposition
2.2
, there is a well-defined action of
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
T
given by the following description: an element
(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{U}(G_{\ell_{i}})
acts by
\rho\in\Aut(T)
if, for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
, the element
g_{i}\in G_{K}
acts on
T
by
\rho
. This construction extends to the situations in which
T
is either profinite or ind-finite by taking limits.

Remark 2.22
.

If
T
is unramified for
\mathcal{U}
-many primes
(\ell_{i})
in any representing sequence of
\mathfrak{u}
, then the action of
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
is also unramified. That is always the case when
\mathfrak{u}
is non-constant and
T
ramifies only at finitely many primes.

2.3
Product of ultraprimes

There is a well-defined notion of products of ultraprimes, obtaining classes in
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
, where
\mathcal{I}_{K}
denotes the set of modulus of
K
. Indeed, for every
s\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a well defined map

\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})^{s}\to\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K}):\ (\mathfrak{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}_{s})\mapsto\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{s}.

This product satisfies a weaker analogue of the unique prime factorisation theorem on
\mathcal{I}_{K}
, since the factorizations are unique but not all of the elements in
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
admit an ultraprime factorization.

Since the product of ultraprimes is commutative, we can express it in the following way.

Definition 2.23
.

The product of ultraprimes is a map

\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})^{s}/S_{s}\to\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K}),

where
S_{s}
denotes the symmetric group, acting on
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
in the natural way. The map is defined as follows: for every finite set of ultraprimes

\mathfrak{u}_{1}=\left(\ell^{(1)}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}_{s}=\left(\ell^{(s)}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}},

we define

\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{s}=(\ell^{1}_{k}\cdots\ell^{(s)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K}).

Remark 2.24
.

Definition
2.23
produces a well-defined class in
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
, which is represented by a sequence
(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
in which
\nu(n_{i})=s
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\nu(I)
denotes the number of prime ideals dividing
I
, counting multiplicities. Hence, the product of
s
ultraprimes cannot be equal to the product of
s^{\prime}
ultraprimes unless
s=s^{\prime}
.

We can show that the map in Definition
2.23
is injective.

Proposition 2.25
.

The product in Definition
2.23
induces an injective map

\bigsqcup_{s>0}:\ \mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})^{s}/S_{s}\to\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K}).

Proof.

Assume that
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{s}=\mathfrak{q}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{q}_{s^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
. By Remark
2.24
, we have that
s=s^{\prime}
. Choose representatives

\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathfrak{u}_{i}=\Bigl(\ell_{k}^{(i)}\Bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{N}},&\mathfrak{q}_{j}=\Bigl(q_{k}^{(j)}\Bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}.\end{array}

By the definition of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
,

\ell_{k}^{(1)}\cdots\ell_{k}^{(s)}=q_{k}^{(1)}\cdots q_{k}^{(s)}\textrm{ for $\mathcal{U}$-many $k$.}

By Proposition
2.2
and the unique factorization in
\mathcal{I}_{K}
, for every
i\leq s
, there is some
j\leq s
such that
\ell_{k}^{(i)}=q_{k}^{(j)}
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
k
, leading to
\mathfrak{u}_{i}=\mathfrak{q}_{j}
. This shows that
(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{q}_{s})
is a reordering of
(\mathfrak{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}_{s})
.
∎

Remark
2.24
and Proposition
2.25
prove the uniqueness of the ultraprime factorization. Now it makes sense to mention square-free products of ultraprimes.

Definition 2.26
.

A product of ultraprimes is called
square-free
if no two
\mathfrak{u}_{i}
are equal.

As mentioned before, the ultraprime factorisation does not exists for every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
, but only when it can be represented by a sequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in which the number of prime divisors of
n_{k}
, counting multiplicities, remains bounded.

Proposition 2.27
.

Assume an element in
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
can be represented by a sequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\nu(n_{k})
is bounded. Then
\mathfrak{n}
can be obtained as a product of ultraprimes.

Assume, in addition, that
n_{k}
is a square-free product for
\mathcal{U}
-many
k
, then
\mathfrak{n}
is also a square-free product.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.2
, there exists
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
S\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\nu(n_{k})=s
for all
k\in S
. It is possible to choose
s
sequences of prime numbers
\Bigl(\ell_{k}^{(j)}\Bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that, whenever
k\in S
, then

n_{k}=\ell_{k}^{(1)}\cdots\ell_{k}^{(s)}.

By construction,
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{s}
, where
\mathfrak{u}_{j}
is the ultraprime represented by the sequence
\Bigl(\ell_{k}^{(j)}\Bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Note that, if
\mathfrak{u}_{i}=\mathfrak{u}_{j}
for some
i
and
j
, every representative
(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{s}
will not contain
\mathcal{U}
-many square-free
n_{i}
, so the last statement holds.
∎

3
Patched cohomology

In this section, we use the ultraproduct defined in the previous one to outline the construction of patched cohomology, which was already constructed in
[
32
]
. Instead of focusing on global patched cohomology, we make this construction in a more general way, so we can also recover local patched cohomology as a particular case.

In order to have control over the patched cohomology groups, one should define it first for finite coefficient rings as an ultraproduct of cohomology groups, and then we extend the definition to either profinite or ind-finite coefficient rings by taking limits.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
T
be a finite, abelian group endowed with an action from every group in a sequence
1
1

1

Technically, it is only needed that the action is well defined for
\mathcal{U}
-many
n
of groups
G=(G_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{U}(\{\textrm{groups}\})
. The
\mathcal{U}
-patched cohomology group is defined as the ultraproduct

\textbf{H}^{i}(G,T):=\mathcal{U}(H^{i}(G_{n},T)).

If
T
is a profinite abelian group, we define the patched cohomology as the inverse limit

\textbf{H}^{i}(G,T):=\varprojlim_{T\twoheadrightarrow T^{\prime}}\textbf{H}^{i}(G,T/T^{\prime}),

where the limit is taken over all the finite quotients of
T
.

Similarly, when
T
is an ind-finite abelian group, the patched cohomology is defined as

\textbf{H}^{i}(G,T):=\varinjlim_{T^{\prime}\hookrightarrow T}\textbf{H}^{i}(G,T^{\prime}),

where the limit is taken over all the finite subgroups of
T
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The assignment

T\mapsto\textbf{H}^{i}(G,T)

is a functor from the category of either finite abelian groups, profinite abelian groups and ind-finite abelian groups to the category of abelian groups.

Proof.

It follows from the functorial properties of cohomology, ultraproducts, and inverse and direct limits.
∎

When the coefficient group
T
is finite, there is a long exact sequence analogously to classical group cohomology.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

be an exact sequence of finite abelian groups. Assume
A
,
B
and
C
are endowed with an action of each group in a sequence
G=(G_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then there is an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(G,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(G,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(G,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(G,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+2}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Proof.

Since
A
,
B
and
C
are finite, we have that

\textbf{H}^{i}(G,-)=\mathcal{U}_{n}\Bigl(H^{i}(G_{n},-)\Bigr).

Then the result follows from Proposition
2.7
and the classical long exact sequence for Galois cohomology.
∎

Under the following assumption, we can construct a long cohomological exact sequence for the patched cohomology with profinite coefficients.

Definition 3.4
.

We say a finite, abelian group
T
endowed with an action of each group in a sequence
G=(G_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the
bounded cohomology property
for some index
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
if there is a constant
K
such that

H^{i}(G_{n},T)\leq K\ \textrm{for $\mathcal{U}$-many $n$.}

Remark 3.5
.

The bounded cohomology property guarantees, using Corollary
2.9
, that the patched cohomology group
\textbf{H}^{1}(G,T)
is finite.

Assuming the bounded cohomology property, we can generalise Proposition
3.3
to profinite groups. The following result is proven
2
2

2

The proof in loc. cit. is done in a slightly different setting, but the argument works even only assuming the bounded cohomology property.
in
[
32
, Lemma 2.4.12]
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

be an exact sequence of continuous maps of profinite abelian groups. Assume
A
,
B
and
C
are endowed with an action of the groups in
G=(G_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and satisfy the bounded cohomology property. Then there is an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(G,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(G,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(G,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(G,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+2}(G,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

3.1
Local patched cohomology

Local patched cohomology groups can be constructed for every ultraprime. It is the patched cohomology of the local Galois groups of the primes in a representing sequence of
\mathfrak{u}
. We will show it coincides with the group cohomology of the Galois group
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
constructed in Definition
2.18
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}
be an ultraprime represented by the sequence
(\ell_{n})
. The local patched cohomology group is defined as

\textbf{H}^{i}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T):=\textbf{H}^{i}\Bigl((G_{K_{\ell_{n}}}),T\Bigr).

Remark 3.8
.

Let
T
be a finite group endowed with an action of the Galois group
G_{K}
that is ramified only at finitely many primes. After fixing inclusions
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
for every prime
\ell
, there is a natural action of
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
T
for every ultrarprime
\mathfrak{u}
. Then
T
satisfies the bounded cohomology property (see Definition
3.4
).

Indeed, if
\mathfrak{u}
is represented by the sequence
(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, then, for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
,
\ell_{i}
does not divide the cardinality of
T
, denoted by
\#T
, and the action of
G_{K_{\ell_{i}}}
on
T
is unramified. For those
i
, local duality implies that

\#H^{1}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\leq 2\#T,

so the bounded cohomology property holds. Then Proposition
3.6
proves the existence of a long exact sequence in the profinite case.

We can mimic the construction to obtain the cohomology of the inertia subgroup. That will lead to finite and singular cohomology groups.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}
be an ultraprime represented by the sequence
(\ell_{n})
. The cohomology of the inertia subgroup
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is defined as the patched cohomology group

\textbf{H}^{i}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T):=\textbf{H}^{i}\Bigl(\mathcal{I}_{\ell_{n}},T\Bigr).

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}
be either a non-constant ultraprime or a constant one that does not ramify on
T
. The
finite patched cohomology group
is defined as

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T):=\ker\biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\biggr).

Remark 3.11
.

Definition
3.10
can be extended to ramifying primes as in
[
29
, Definition I.3.4]
.

Definition 3.12
.

We define the
singular patched cohomology group
as

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T):=\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}{\textbf{H}_{\textrm{f}}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}.

The local patched cohomology coincides with the group cohomology of the local Galois group defined in Definition
2.18
.

Proposition 3.13
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.3.2]
)
Let
T
be either a finite, profinite or ind-finite abelian group, and let
\mathfrak{u}
be an ultraprime represented by the sequence
(\ell_{n})
. Then

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textbf{H}^{i}\Bigl(G_{\ell_{k}},T\Bigr)=H^{i}(G_{\mathfrak{u}},T),&\textbf{H}^{i}\Bigl(\mathcal{I}_{\ell_{k}},T\Bigr)=H^{i}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T),\end{array}

where
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}
are the patched local Galois group defined in Definition
2.18
. Recall that
H
denotes patched cohomology while
H
denotes standard group cohomology.

The study of local patched cohomology groups can take advantage of the following fact.

Corollary 3.14
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.3.2]
)
Let
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{n})
be an ultraprime and let
T
be a finite group endowed with an action of
G_{K}
. For
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
, there are isomorphisms

\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi_{i}^{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong H^{1}(K_{\ell_{i}},T),&\varphi_{i}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{\ell_{i}},T),\\
\varphi_{i}^{\mathfrak{u},\textrm{f}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong H^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T),&\varphi_{i}^{\mathfrak{u},\textrm{s}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong H^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T).\end{array}

Patched cohomology also satisfies an analogue of local duality.

Notation 3.15
.

If
T
is a Galois representation, its Cartier dual will be denoted by
T^{*}
. Similarly, for any group
M
, its Pontryagin dual is denoted by
M^{\vee}
.

Proposition 3.16
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.6.4]
)
Let
T
be either a finite, profinite or ind-finite Galois module and let
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an ultraprime, which is not a constant archimedean prime. For
i=0,1,2
, there is a non-degenerate pairing

\textbf{H}^{i}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\times\textbf{H}^{2-i}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

Under this pairing,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
and
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})
are annihilators of each other.

3.2
Global patched cohomology

The goal of this section is to outline the construction of global patched cohomology groups. We do this by generalising the notion of Galois cohomology of the maximal extension
K^{\Sigma}/K
unramified outside the certain finite set of prime numbers
\Sigma
. Hence, we aim to construct the patched cohomology group unramified outside a finite set of ultraprimes.

Definition 3.17
.

(
[
32
, Definition 2.4.2]
)
Let
T
be either a finite, profinite or ind-finite abelian group endowed with an action of
G_{K}
that is ramified outside finitely many primes. Let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
be a square-free product of ultraprimes represented by the sequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. We define the maximal patched cohomology group unramified at
\mathfrak{n}
by

\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T):=\textbf{H}^{i}\Bigl(\Gal(K^{n_{k}}/K),T^{G_{K^{n_{k}}}}\Bigr),

where
K^{n_{k}}
represents the maximal extension of
K
unramified outside the prime divisors of
n_{k}
. Note that this definition is independent of the sequence representing
\mathfrak{n}
.

Notation 3.18
.

If
\Sigma
is a finite set of distinct ultraprimes and
\mathfrak{n}
is the product of all the ultraprimes in
\Sigma
, we will also denote
\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)
by
\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T)
.

The most important property of this construction is that the global patched cohomology groups satisfy the bonded cohomology property from Definition
3.4
. In order to show it, we take advantage of the fact that every sequence
(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
representing the product of ultraprimes
\mathfrak{n}
satisfies that
\nu(n_{i})=k
is constant for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 3.19
.

(
[
32
, Lemma 2.4.5]
, see also
[
23
, Theorem 4.10]
)
Let
T
be a finite abelian group endowed with an action of
G_{K}
that is ramified outside finitely many primes and let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{I}_{K})
is a finite product of ultraprimes. Denote by
n^{\prime}
the square-free product of constant prime divisors of
\mathfrak{n}
. Then
T^{\prime}:=T^{\Gal(K^{n^{\prime}}/K)}
and the natural action of

G(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K):=(\Gal(K^{n_{i}}/K))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

satisfy the bounded cohomology property stated in Definition
3.4
. Note that the action is only defined when
n_{i}
is not divisible by any archimedean or
p
-adic primes or any primes at which
T
is ramified. However, the conditions on
T
and the fact that no constant ultraprimes divides
\mathfrak{n}
implies that it is defined for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
.

Proof.

Say that
\mathfrak{n}
is the product of
S
ultraprimes. Then
\nu(n_{i})=s
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
. Then the proposition follows from
[
32
, Lemma 2.4.5]
(see also
[
23
, Theorem 4.10]
).
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
T
be a finite group and let
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
be a finite set of ultraprimes. The patched cohomology groups
\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T)
are finite for all
i\geq 0
.

Global patched cohomology also satisfies a Tate’s formula for the Euler characteristic.

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be a square-free product of ultraprimes. Then the sequence
\Gal(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K)
has (patched)
p
-cohomological dimension
2
and for any finite group
T
endowed with an action of
\Gal(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K)
, we have that

\frac{\#\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)\#\textbf{H}^{0}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)}{\#\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)}=\prod_{v\in\Sigma_{\infty}}\frac{\#\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{v},T)}{\#T},

(1)

where
\Sigma_{\infty}
denotes the set of archimedean places of
K
.

Proof.

Let
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence representing
\mathfrak{n}
. Since
\Gal(K^{n_{k}}/K)
has
p
-cohomological dimension
2
by
[
24
, Proposition 8.3.18]
, then
\Gal(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K)
also has cohomological dimension
2
. In addition, Corollary
2.9
and Proposition
3.19
imply the existence of
\mathcal{U}
-many
k
such that

\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)\cong H^{i}(K^{n_{k}}/K,T)\ \forall k=0,1,2.

Then (
1
) holds from its classical analogue in
[
24
, Theorem 8.7.4]
.
∎

The following corollary holds by combining Propositions
3.6
and
3.19
.

Corollary 3.22
.

Let

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

be an exact sequence of continuous maps of profinite
G_{K}
-modules that are unramified outside finitely many primes. If
\Sigma
denotes a finite set of ultraprimes that does not contain any ramifying prime of
T
, there is an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\Sigma}/K,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\Sigma}/K,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\Sigma}/K,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{i+2}(K^{\Sigma}/K,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Remark 3.23
.

If
\Sigma_{1}\subset\Sigma_{2}\subset\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
are two finite sets of ultraprimes. Then there is a natural map

\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\Sigma_{1}}/K,T)\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{i}(K^{\Sigma_{2}}/K,T).

When
T
is finite, it is induced by a sequence of inflation maps. The general case, when
T
is profinite and ind-finite, follows by taking limits.

We can use the maps in Remark
3.23
to construct the absolute global patched cohomology group.

Definition 3.24
.

(
[
32
, §2.4.6]
)
The absolute global patched cohomology group is defined as

\textbf{H}^{1}(K,T):=\varinjlim_{\Sigma\subset\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})}\textbf{H}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T),

where the limit is taken over all the finite sets and the transition maps are the ones defined in Remark
3.23
.

Remark 3.25
.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of ultraprimes and let
\mathfrak{u}
be an ultraprime. There exists a restriction map

\res:\ \textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T),

induced, when
T
is finite, by the restriction map in every factor of the ultraproduct. When
T
is profinite (resp. ind-finite), the restriction map is obtained as the limit of the restriction map in the cohomology of the finite quotients (resp. submodules).

The patched cohomology unramified outside a set of primes
\Sigma
can be recovered as the unramified part of the absolute global patched cohomology group.

Proposition 3.26
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.4.11]
)
Let
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
be a finite set of ultraprimes. Then

\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T)=\ker\left(\textbf{H}^{1}(K,T)\to\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})\setminus\Sigma}\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}\right).

Local and glocal patched cohomology also satisfy an analogue of Poitou-Tate
9
-term exact sequence.

Proposition 3.27
.

Let
T
be either a finite, profinite or ind-finite group endowed with an action of
G_{K}
and let
\Sigma
be a finite set of ultraprimes containing all the archimedean ones, p-adic ones and all ramifying primes of
T
. Then there is a
9
-term exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma}}\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma}}\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma}}\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{0}(K^{\Sigma}/K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Proof.

When
T
is finite, it follows from Poitou-Tate duality in classical Galois cohomology (
[
24
, Theorem 8.4.4]
) and the exactness of the ultraproduct in Proposition
2.7
. When
T
is either profinite or ind-finite, it follows by taking limits.
∎

4
Patched Selmer groups

4.1
Patched Selmer structures

Following
[
32
]
, we can now define the Selmer structures in this setting. The main innovation is that they also include local conditions at non-constant ultraprimes. However, classical Selmer structures can be recovered when the local condition is the finite cohomology group for every non-constant ultraprime.

Definition 4.1
.

A
Selmer structure

\mathcal{F}
consists of the following data:

•

A finite set
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
containing all constant ultraprimes lying over
p
,
\infty
and ramified (constant) ultraprimes of
T
.

•

For each
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
, a closed
R
-submodule

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\subset\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T).

Definition 4.2
.

The Selmer module of a Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}
is defined as

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T):=\ker\Biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/K,T)\to\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}\Biggr).

Remark 4.3
.

By Proposition
3.26
, a Selmer structure depends only on the local conditions. If we set
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T):=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
whenever
\mathfrak{u}\notin\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
, then

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)=\ker\Biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}(K,T)\to\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})}\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}\Biggr).

Local conditions propagate to quotients and submodules in the following way.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\ T\hookrightarrow T^{\prime\prime}
be a quotient map. For every ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}
, it induces a map

\varepsilon:\ \textbf{H}^{1}(K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}(K,T^{\prime\prime})

A local condition at
T
propagates to
T^{\prime}
as

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{\prime\prime}):=\varepsilon\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\Bigr).

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\ T^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow T
be a submodule. For every ultraprimes
\mathfrak{u}
, this inclusion induces a map

\mu:\ \textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{\prime})\to\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T).

A local condition at
T
propagates to
T^{\prime}
as

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{\prime}):=\mu^{-1}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\Bigr).

Then Selmer groups can be recovered as the limit of the propagated Selmer groups with finite coefficients.

Proposition 4.6
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.5.6]
)
Let
T
be a countably profinite abelian group and let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure defined on
T
. Then

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)=\varprojlim_{T\twoheadrightarrow T^{\prime}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{\prime}),

where the limit is taken over the finite quotients
T^{\prime}
of
T
and the Selmer group
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{\prime})
is obtained from the propagated Selmer structure, as defined in Definition
4.4
.

Proposition 4.7
.

(
[
32
, Proposition 2.5.6]
)
Let
T
be an ind-finite group and let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure defined on
T
. Then

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)=\varinjlim_{T^{\prime}\hookrightarrow T}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{\prime}),

where the limit is taken over the finite submodules
T^{\prime}
of
T
and
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T^{\prime})
is the Selmer group of the propagated Selmer structure, as defined in Definition
4.5
.

4.2
Dual patched Selmer structures

In this section, we extend the theory in
[
32
]
to construct a global duality exact sequence of patched Selmer groups.

Definition 4.8

(Dual Selmer structure)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure on
T
. We can define a
dual Selmer structure

\mathcal{F}^{*}
on the Cartier dual
T^{*}
by defining the local condition
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})
as the annihilator of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
under the local duality pairing in Proposition
3.16

This construction can be used to obtain a global duality exact sequence in the patched setting.

Proposition 4.9
.

(Global duality)
Let
\mathcal{F}\leq\mathcal{G}
be two Selmer structures. Then there is a global duality exact sequence (recall Notation
3.15
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}}\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Proof.

Assume first that
T
is finite. Let
\mathfrak{n}=(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the square-free product of all ultraprimes in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}
. By Lemma
4.10
below, there are sequences of classical Selmer structures
(\mathcal{F}_{i})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{i})
, where
\mathcal{F}_{i}\leq\mathcal{G}_{i}
, and

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\Bigr),\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\Bigr).\end{array}

In addition, Lemma
4.10
below also gives that

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr),\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr).\end{array}

By Proposition
2.7
and Lemma
4.11
below, the dual Selmer groups can be also obtained as an ultraproduct of classical Selmer groups:

\displaystyle\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}_{i})^{*}}(K,T^{*})\Bigr),

\displaystyle\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}(K,T^{*})=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{(\mathcal{G}_{i})^{*}}(K,T^{*})\Bigr).

Then the global duality exact sequence is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\left(\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\ell_{i}\mid n_{i}}\frac{H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)}{H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

which is exact by Proposition
2.7
.

When
T
is profinite (resp. ind-finite), then Proposition
4.6
(resp. Proposition
4.7
) implies that the above exact sequence can be obtained as the inverse (resp. direct) limit of the associated exact sequences for the finite quotients (resp. submodules) of
T
. The proof is thus concluded since the inverse limit of finite groups (resp. direct limit) is an exact functor.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\leq\mathcal{G}
be two Selmer structures defined on a finite Galois module
T
. Then there are sequences of classical Selmer structures
(\mathcal{F}_{i})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{i})
such that
\mathcal{F}_{i}\leq\mathcal{G}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and for any representative
(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}
,

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\Bigr),\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\Bigr).\end{array}

Moreover, we can construct the patched Selmer group as the ultraproduct of classical Selmer groups

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr),\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr).\end{array}

Proof.

For an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}
, we have fixed a representing sequence
(\ell_{i})
. We can assume, without loss of generality, that no prime appears in two different sequences on the same entry. Let
W_{\mathfrak{u}}
be the set of indices such that there is an isomorphism

\varphi_{i}^{\mathfrak{u}}:\ H^{1}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\cong\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T).

Note that Corollary
3.14
implies that
W_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\mathcal{U}
. For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, define the classical Selmer structure by the set of primes

\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}=\{\ell_{i}:\ \mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}\}

and local conditions

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)=(\varphi_{i}^{\mathfrak{u}})^{-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)

\displaystyle\textrm{ if $i\in W_{\mathfrak{u}}$,}

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)=0

\displaystyle\textrm{ if $i\notin W_{\mathfrak{u}}$.}

This definition implies that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\Bigr)
. Therefore, Proposition
2.7
implies that

\displaystyle\ker\Biggl[\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)\to\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{n}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\Biggr]=

\displaystyle\ker\left[\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}(K^{n_{i}}/K,T)\Bigr)\to\mathcal{U}_{i}\left(\prod_{\ell_{i}\mid n_{i}}H^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\right)\right].

Again, the exactness of the ultraproduct given in Proposition
2.7
implies that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr).

Similarly, define Selmer structures
\mathcal{G}_{i}
by the set of primes

\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}=\{\ell_{i}:\ \mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}\}

and local conditions

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)=(\varphi_{i}^{\mathfrak{u}})^{-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)

\displaystyle\textrm{ if $i\in W_{\mathfrak{u}}$,}

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)=H^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}_{i}},T)

\displaystyle\textrm{ if $i\notin W_{\mathfrak{u}}$.}

By construction
\mathcal{F}_{i}\leq\mathcal{G}_{i}
and, analogously,

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{G}}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}(K,T)\Bigr).\qed

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
a patched Selmer structure defined on a finite group
T
and let
\mathcal{F}_{i}
be classical Selmer structures such that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\Bigr)

for every ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then, we have that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}^{*}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T^{*})\Bigr).

Proof.

By Definition
4.8
, for every ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})=\ker\left(H^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})\to\Hom\left(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T),\frac{(\#T)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\right),

where the map is induced by local duality, as stated in Proposition
3.16
. This map can be also written as the composition

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}(K_{\ell_{i}},T^{*})\Bigr)\to\mathcal{U}_{i}\left(\Hom\left(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T),\frac{(\#T)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\right)=

\displaystyle\Hom\left(\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T)\Bigr),\mathcal{U}\left(\frac{(\#T)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\right),

where the equality follows from Proposition
2.11
. By local Tate duality for Galois cohomology and
2.7
, we can compute the kernel of this map as

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})=\mathcal{U}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}^{*}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T^{*})\Bigr).\qed

4.3
Selmer modules

For the rest of this article, we will assume that
T
admits an
R
-module structure where
R
is a ring satisfying the following assumption.

Assumption (Reg)
.

Let
R
be a commutative, complete, noetherian, regular local domain with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
and finite residue field
k
of characteristic
p
.

Remark 4.12
.

By Auslander-Buchsbaum theorem, we now that
R
is a unique factorisation domain. Fix, once and forall, a regular system of parameters
\{\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}\}
. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
, define the ideal and the quotient

\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{k}:=(\pi_{1}^{k},\ldots,\pi_{n}^{k}),&R_{k}=R/I_{k}\end{array}

Since
R
is complete, it can be recovered as the inverse limit.

R=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}R_{k},

Since
R
is a unique factorisation domain,
R_{k}[\mathfrak{m}]
is a one-dimensional
k
-vector space, generated by
(\pi_{1}^{k-1}\cdots\pi_{n}^{k-1})
.

Remark 4.13
.

For every
m<n
, the ideal
R_{n}[I_{m}]
is a principal ideal of
R_{n}
. Indeed, since
R_{n}
is self-injective, it can be seen as

R_{n}[I_{m}]:=\ker\left(\Hom(R_{n},R_{n})\to\Hom(I_{m},R_{n})\right)=\Hom(R_{m},R_{n})

Since
R_{m}
is self-injective, then
R_{m}[\mathfrak{m}]
is a one-dimensional
k
-vector space. Hence
R_{n}[I_{m}]/\mathfrak{m}
is also one-dimensional as a
k
-vector space, so Nayayama’s lemma implies that
R_{n}[I_{m}]
is principal.

Notation 4.14
.

In addition, let
T
be a
R[[G_{K}]]
-module that is finitely generated as an
R
-module, in which the Galois action ramifies only at finitely many primes. Similarly, we denote
T_{k}=T\otimes_{R}R_{k}
. It is important to note that, since
R
is complete and
T
is finitely generated, then
T_{k}
is a cofinal sequence, so

T=\varprojlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}T_{k}.

The advantage of patched cohomology is that we can develop a theory of Kolyvagin systems for infinite rings, such as those satisfying Assumption
(Reg)
. That was not possible in the classical setting, since the finiteness of the ring
R
was a requirement for constructing Kolyvagin primes using the Chebotarev density theorem. In order to develop the theory of Kolyvagin systems, we need to make the standard big image assumption.

Assumption (BI)
.

In addition, we assume the following assumptions:

•

(T1)
T/\mathfrak{m}T
is an irreducible
k[[G_{K}]]
-module.

•

(T2) There exists
\tau\in G_{K(\mu_{p^{\infty}})}
such that
T/(\tau-1)T\cong R
as
R
-modules.

•

(T3)
H^{1}(K(T)_{p^{\infty}}/K,T)=H^{1}(K(T)_{p^{\infty}}/K,T^{*}(1))=0
, where
K(T)_{p^{\infty}}
is the minimal extension
F/K(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
such that
G_{F}
acts trivially on
T
.

Remark 4.15
.

By
•
‣
(BI)
, we know that
(T/\mathfrak{m}T)^{G_{K}}
is either
0
or
T/\mathfrak{m}T
. However, the latter is not possible since it does not satisfy
•
‣
(BI)
.

That implies that
(T/IT)^{G_{K}}=0
for every ideal
I
of
R
and, dualizing,
(T^{*}[I])^{G_{K}}
. It can be also seen in terms of patched cohomology with the equalities

\textbf{H}^{0}(K,T/I)=\textbf{H}^{0}(K,T^{*}[I])=0.

We can use this to show that, under these assumptions, profinite Selmer groups are torsion free.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
T
be a Galois representation satisfying Assumption
(BI)
. For every Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}
, the Selmer group

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)

is a torsion-free
R
-module.

Proof.

Let
a
in
R
and consider the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T/aT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

By Corollary
3.22
and Remark
4.15
, we have that

\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/K,T)[a]=0.

Since that holds for every
a\in R
and Definition
4.1
defines the Selmer group as a subgroup of the global patched cohomology group, we can deduce that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)_{\textrm{tors}}=0.\qed

4.4
Cartesian Selmer structures and core rank

In order to develop this theory, we need to impose the cartesian condition on Selmer structures, as defined below.

Definition 4.17

(Cartesian local condition)
.

A local condition
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
is called
cartesian
if the quotient

\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}

is a torsion-free
R
-module. A Selmer structure is said to be cartesian if all of its local conditions are cartesian.

Remark 4.18
.

The connection of Definition
4.17
with the cartesian condition appearing in the previous theory of Kolyvagin systems, first introduced in
[
21
, Definition 1.1.4]
lies in
[
21
, Lemma 3.7.1]
, where it is proven that both definitions are equivalent when
R
is a discrete valuation ring. When
R
is a more complicated ring, such as the Iwasawa algebra, Definition
4.17
is a milder assumption (see Proposition
9.3
below).

Remark 4.19
.

In order to check that a Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}
is cartesian, it is enough to see that local conditions are cartesians for ultraprimes
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
. Indeed, finite cohomology groups are always cartesian local conditions since

\textbf{H}^{1}_{s}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\subset\Hom(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T)

is
R
-torsion-free since
R
is a domain and
T
is a free module.

Remark 4.20
.

Considering the computations in Proposition
9.3
below when
R
is the Iwasawa algebra, it might seem reasonable to define cartesian conditions imposing that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
is a free
R
-module. However, that definition presents some technical issues since we cannot guarantee that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
is a free module in this generality .

The main advantage of cartesian structures is that they allow the study of Selmer groups of
T
by projecting to the cohomology of
T_{1}
. In order to do that, we assume the following mild assumption.

Assumption (Proj)
.

There is a square-free product of ultraprimes
\mathfrak{n}
, divisible by all the primes in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
, such that the following projection map is injective:

\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}},T)\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T_{1}).

Remark 4.21
.

When Assumption
(BI)
hold and
R
is a principal ring, then Assumption
(Proj)
also holds for every square-free product of ultraprimes
\mathfrak{n}
. Indeed, Remark
4.15
implies that
\textbf{H}^{0}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)=0
for every square-free product of ultraprimes
\mathfrak{n}
. Then the short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

induces, by Corollary
3.22
, an injection

\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T_{1}).

Remark 4.22
.

When
R
is the Iwasawa algebra, it will be shown in Proposition
5.2
that Assumption
(Proj)
is equivalent to the absence finite submodules in the local cohomology groups.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure. If
\mathfrak{n}
is a square-free product of all the ultraprimes in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
, for every ideal
I
of
R
, there is an injection

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)/I\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)/I.

(2)

Proof.

By Definition
4.1
and Remark
4.3
, there is an injection

\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)}\hookrightarrow\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{n}}\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}.

Then the cartesian condition in Definition
4.17
implies that

\left(\frac{\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)}{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)}\right)[I]=0.

(3)

Then the proof is completed by Lemma
4.24
below.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
\mu:\ A\hookrightarrow B
be an injection of
R
-modules such that the quotient
B/\mu(A)
is torsion free. Then, for every ideal
I\subset R
, the map
\mu
induces an injection

\overline{\mu}:\ A/I\hookrightarrow B/I.

Proof.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
\overline{\mu}
is not injective. Then there exists some
a\in A
such that

\begin{array}[]{cc}a\notin IA,&\mu(a)\in IB.\end{array}

Then there exists some
b\in B
and
\lambda\in I
such that
\mu(a)=\lambda b
. Since
a
is not
I
-divisible in
A
, then
b\notin\mu(A)
. Hence
b+\mu(A)
induces a non-trivial
I
-torsion class in the quotient
B/\mu(A)
, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 4.25
.

If Assumption
(Proj)
holds and
\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure, the following projection map is injective.

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1}).

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be the square-free product of ultraprimes given by Assumption
(Proj)
. Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T_{1}).}

In this diagram, the left vertical map is injective by Proposition
2
and the bottom horizontal map is injective by Assumption
(Proj)
. Hence the top horizontal map needs to be injective.
∎

The previous corollary implies that the analogous maps induced by the quotients
R_{k}
are also injective.

Proposition 4.26
.

If Assumption
(Proj)
hold and
\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure, the map

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\otimes_{R}R_{k}\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})

is injective for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Let

x\in\ker\biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\biggr).

Then,
x
is also in the kernel of the projection map to
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1})
, so Corollary
4.25
implies the existence of an element
a\in\mathfrak{m}
and
y\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
such that
x=ay
.

We repeat this process with the class
y
until we find an element that is not in the kernel of the projection map to
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1})
, so we construct an element
z\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
such that
x=bz
for some
b\in R
. We aim to show that
b\in I_{k}
. Note that, in case that the condition on
z
is never achieved, we will eventually find an element
b\in I_{k}
, which would prove the proposition.

Assume by contradiction that it is not and let
\overline{b}
be the projection in
R_{k}
. By assumption,

\Pi(z)\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})[\overline{b}],

where
\Pi
represents the canonical projection map. However, since
\overline{b}\neq 0
divides any element in
R_{k}[\mathfrak{m}]
, Lemma
4.27
below implies that the projection of
z
to the Selmer group of
T_{1}
vanishes, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
I=R_{k}[\mathfrak{m}]
. Then the following holds:

\ker\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1})\Bigr)=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})[I],

where the map is induced by the projection map
T_{k}\twoheadrightarrow T_{1}
.

Proof.

Since
R_{k}
is self-injective,
I
is the minimal non-zero ideal of
R_{k}
, so it is principal. Choose a generator
x\in I
. Since
T_{k}
is a free
R_{k}
-module, there is a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[x]}
\textstyle{T_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{k}/T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
T_{k}/T_{1}=T/IT
, where
I
is seen as an ideal of
R
containing
I_{k}
, Corollary
3.22
and Remark
4.15
induces an injection

[x]:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1})\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k}).

Note that the following composition is the multiplication by
x
:

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[x]}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k}).}

Since the second map is injective, then

\ker\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{1})\Bigr)=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})[x].\qed

The next result can be seen as an analogue of the previous theory in the dual representation
T^{*}
, concerning submodules instead of quotients. Its prove is the same as the one in
[
4
, Proposition 3.5 and Corollary 3.8]

Proposition 4.28
.

(
[
4
, Corollary 3.8]
)
Under Assumptions
(BI)
, for every finitely generated ideal
I
of
R
, the inclusion
T^{*}[I]\hookrightarrow T^{*}
induces an isomorphism

H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*}[I])\cong H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})[I].

We can now define the concept of core rank. We give a definition that applies for any Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}
, not necessarily cartesian.

Definition 4.29
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure. We define the
core rank
of
\mathcal{F}
as

\chi(\mathcal{F}):=\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)-\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}.

Remark 4.30
.

The core rank is well defined since
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
and
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}
are both finitely generated modules.

4.5
Kolyvagin ultraprimes

In the patched cohomology setting, we can define Kolyvagin ultraprimes in a similar way as was done in
[
21
]
. Recall that Assumption
(BI)
implies the existence of
\tau\in G_{K_{p^{\infty}}}
such that
T/(\tau-1)
is a free
R
-module of rank one. Once and for all, we fix an automorphism
\tau\in G_{K}
satisfying this assumption.

Definition 4.31
.

An ultraprime is said to be a
Kolyvagin ultraprime
if
\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is conjugate to
\tau
in
\Gal(K(T)_{p^{\infty}}/K)
.

Notation 4.32
.

We denote by
\mathcal{P}(T)
the set of Kolyvagin ultraprimes, and we denote by
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{P})
and
\mathcal{N}_{i}(\mathcal{P})
the set of square-free products of Kolyvagin primes and exactly
i
Kolyvagin primes, respectively. When it is clear from the context, we will also denote them by
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{N}
or
\mathcal{N}_{i}
.

Notation 4.33
.

For
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, we denote by
\nu(\mathfrak{n})
the number of ultraprimes dividing
\mathfrak{n}
.

Remark 4.34
.

The existence of Kolyvagin ultraprimes is guaranteed by Proposition
2.17
. Note that
K(T)_{p^{\infty}}/K
might be an infinite extension, so we cannot apply the classical version of the Chebotarev density theorem to guarantee the existence of Kolyvagin primes.

We can describe explicitly the local cohomology of Kolyvagin ultraprimes.

Proposition 4.35
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}
be a Kolyvagin ultraprime. Then there is a homomorphism

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong T/(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1)T\cong T/(\tau-1)T\cong R.

Proof.

Recall that
T_{k}=T\otimes_{R}R_{k}
and let
(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be the Kolyvagin ultraprime. For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
the minimal natural number such that
p^{\alpha_{k}}R_{k}=0
.

By construction, for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
, the Frobenius element
\textrm{Frob}_{\ell_{i}}
is conjugate to
\tau
in
\Gal(K(T_{k})_{p^{\alpha_{n}}}/K)
. Therefore,
\ell_{i}
is a Kolyvagin prime for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
. For those
i
,
[
21
, Lemma 1.2.1]
implies that

H^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k})\cong\frac{T_{k}}{(\textrm{Frob}_{\ell_{i}}-1)T_{k}}\cong\frac{T_{k}}{(\tau-1)T_{k}}\cong R_{k}.

By Proposition
2.2
, we obtain that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T_{k})=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k})\Bigr)\cong\frac{T_{k}}{(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1)T_{k}}\cong\frac{T_{k}}{(\tau-1)T_{k}}\cong R_{k}.

Taking limits, we get that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong\frac{T}{(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1)T}\cong\frac{T}{(\tau-1)T}\cong R.\qed

We can use a similar argument for constructing the patched transverse local conditions.

Definition 4.36
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a Kolyvagin ultraprime. For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
K(\ell_{i})
the maximal
p
-extension inside the ray class field modulo
\ell_{i}
. We define the transverse local condition as

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{U}_{i}\biggl(\textrm{Im}\left(\inf:\ H^{1}(K(\ell_{i})_{\ell_{i}}/K,T_{k})\to H^{1}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k})\right)\biggr).

We can now prove the basic properties of the transverse cohomology used to develop the theory of Kolyvagin systems.

Proposition 4.37
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a Kolyvagin ultraprime. Then the transverse cohomology group is a free, cyclic
R
-module and the local patched cohomology splits as

\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\oplus\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T).

Proof.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
S_{k}
the set of indices such that
\ell_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(T_{k})
. Note that
S_{k}\in\mathcal{U}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For
i\in S_{k}
,
[
21
, Lemmas 1.2.1 and 1.2.4]
imply that

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k})\cong R_{k},&\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k})\cong\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k})\oplus\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k}).\end{array}

By Lemma
2.8
, the finite patched version also satisfies that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T_{k})=\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(H^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\ell_{i}},T_{k})\Bigr)\cong R_{k}.

(4)

Taking limits, we obtain that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T_{k})\cong\varprojlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{k}=R.

It is worth mentioning that, since the isomorphisms in (
4
) are not canonical, we cannot guarantee that the transition maps in both limits coincide. However, it is possible to guarantee that the transition maps are surjective, with implies that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
is a free module of rank one.

Remark
2.10
implies that

\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T_{k})\cong\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T_{k})\oplus\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T_{k}).

And, taking limits,

\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\oplus\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T).\qed

Remark 4.38
.

Note that Proposition
4.37
gives a canonical isomorphism

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T).

We can also construct a finite-singular map in the patched setting. In order to do that, we need to define a generalisation of the Galois groups
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}:=\Gal(K(\ell)/K)
for ultraprimes.

Definition 4.39
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{M}_{K})
be an ultraprime. Define the group

\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}:=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{U}_{i}\Bigl(\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{i}}/p^{k}\Bigr).

Remark 4.40
.

Note that Definition
4.39
is independent of the sequence representing the ultraprime. In addition, if
\mathfrak{u}
is the constant ultraprime
(\ell)
, then
\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}
coincides with the group
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
.

Remark 4.41
.

If
\mathfrak{u}
is a Kolyvagin ultraprime, then
G_{\mathfrak{u}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Proposition 4.42
.

There is a canonical isomorphism

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong\Hom(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T)^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}.

Proof.

This is obtained from the inflation-restriction sequence, in the identifications of Proposition
3.13
, and the cohomological dimension of
\langle\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}\rangle
being one.
∎

We can restate the isomorphism in Proposition
4.42
in the following way:

Corollary 4.43
.

If
\mathfrak{u}
