is a Kolyvagin ultraprime, there is a canonical isomorphism

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}\cong T^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}.

Proof.

By Definition
2.18
, there is an isomorphism

\Hom(\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\to\Hom(\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1),T).

Since
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Kolyvagin ultraprime, then there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{i}}/p^{k}=\mu_{p^{k}}
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Those isomorphisms can be patched to give a canonical identification
\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\mu_{p^{\infty}}
. Moreover, the fact that
\mathfrak{u}
is a Kolyvagin prime implies that
\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}
acts trivially on the
p
-adic roots of unit, so
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1)
are the same as
\langle\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}\rangle
-modules. By Proposition
4.42

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}\cong\Hom(\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1),T)^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}=T^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}.\qed

Finite-singular maps also appear in this setting.

Proposition 4.44
.

If
\mathfrak{u}
is a Kolyvagin ultraprime, there is a canonical isomorphism, known as
finite-singular map
,

\phi^{\textrm{fs}}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}.

Proof.

Let
P_{\mathfrak{u}}
be the characteristic polynomial of the Galois action of
\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}
on
T
. The fact that
T/(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1)
is one dimensional, implies that
P_{\mathfrak{u}}(X)=(X-1)Q_{\mathfrak{u}}(X)
, where
Q_{\mathfrak{u}}
is a polynomial satisfying that
Q_{\mathfrak{u}}(1)\neq 0
. Note that, since
P_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}})
acts trivially on
T
, then
Q_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}})
induces a map

Q_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}):\ T/(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1)T\to T^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}.

Consider then the composition,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.53297pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.53297pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.53297pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.53297pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T/(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1)T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 147.58156pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 147.58156pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 215.73103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 215.73103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The first and third maps are isomorphisms by Proposition
4.35
and Corollary
4.43
. Hence it only remains to see that
Q_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}})
induces an isomorphism. Since
T/\mathfrak{m}
is an
R/\mathfrak{m}
-vector space, the following map is an isomorphism:

\overline{Q_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}})}:\ T/(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1,\mathfrak{m})T\to(T/\mathfrak{m})^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}.

Nakayama’s lemma then implies that
Q_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}})
is surjective. Since
T/(\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}-1)T
and
T^{\textrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{u}}=1}
are free
R
-modules of the same rank, then it is in fact an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 4.45
.

Note that, after choosing a generator of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}
, the finite-singular map can be written as a well-determined map

\phi^{\textrm{fs}}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T).

Note that, when
\mathfrak{u}
is represented by the sequence
(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, then choosing a generator of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is the same as choosing a generator of
\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{i}}
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
. In this case, the finite-singular maps
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}^{\textrm{fs}}
can be obtained by patching
\phi_{\ell_{i}}^{\textrm{fs}}
in a natural way.

We can now use these local conditions to modify Selmer structures, analogously to
[
21
, Example 2.8]

Definition 4.46
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure and let
\mathfrak{a}
,
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{c}
be pairwise coprime square-free products of ultraprimes. Assume
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
. Define the Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{c})
by the local conditions

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{c})}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0\ &\textrm{if }\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{a}\\
&\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\ &\textrm{if }\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{b}\\
&\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\ &\textrm{if }\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{c}\\
&\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\ &\textrm{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

Modified Selmer structures are cartesian and their core rank varies in a controlled way.

Proposition 4.47
.

(
[
30
, Corollary 3.21]
)
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure and let
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
be pairwise coprime. Then
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{c})
is also cartesian and

\chi(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{c}))=\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{b})-\nu(\mathfrak{a}).

Proof.

For every Kolyvagin prime
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
, both
\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
and
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
are torsion-free, so
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{c})
is cartesian.

Proposition
4.9
produces an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}}(K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Since the rank is additive, then
\chi\bigl(\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}\bigr)=\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{b})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})
. Proposition
4.9
produces another exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}}(K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{c}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Thus,

\chi\bigl(\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathfrak{c})\bigr)=\chi\bigl(\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}\bigr)-\nu(\mathfrak{a})-\nu(\mathfrak{c})=\chi\bigl(\mathcal{F}\bigr)+\nu(\mathfrak{b})-\nu(\mathfrak{a}).\qed

The analogue of
[
21
, Lemma 4.1.7(ii)]
is a central technical result when working with Kolyvagin systems.

Lemma 4.48
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure and let
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
be an ultraprime satisfying that

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)

is surjective. Then
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,T^{*})=H^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})
.

Proof.

The surjectivity of
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}
, together with the exact sequence of Proposition
4.9
with Selmer structures
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\mathcal{F}
implies that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*}).

(5)

By construction, we have that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})\cap\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,T^{*}).

By (
5
),

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})\cap\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,T^{*}).

Since
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,T^{*})\subset\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})
, we get that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,T^{*}).\qed

4.6
Chebotarev density theorem

This section is devoted to proving analogues of
[
4
, Lemma 3.9]
in the patched cohomology formalism. In the most general setting, we only need to work under Assumption
(Reg)
on the coefficient ring
R
.

Proposition 4.49
.

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be a square-free product of ultraprimes, let

c_{1},\ldots,c_{s}\in\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)\setminus\mathfrak{m}\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)

and let

d_{1},\ldots,d_{t}\in\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T^{*})\setminus\{0\}.

Under Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Proj)
, if
s+t<p
, there exists an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(c_{i})
generates
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
for all
i=1,\ldots,s
and
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(d_{j})\neq 0
for all
j=1,\ldots,t
.

Proof.

Write

c_{i}=(c_{i})_{k}\in\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T_{k}).

Since
c_{i}\notin\mathfrak{m}\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)
, Assumption
(Proj)
implies that
(c_{i})_{1}
does not vanish. Fix a sequence
\Bigl((c_{i})_{1}^{(k)}\Bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
representing
(c_{i})_{1}
in

\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T_{1})=\mathcal{U}_{k}\Bigl(H^{1}(K^{n_{k}}/K,T_{1})\Bigr).

where
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence representing
\mathfrak{n}
. Similarly, there exists
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that

d_{j}\in\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T^{*}_{a})\ \forall j=1,\ldots,t.

Similarly, fix sequences
\Bigl(d_{j}^{(k)}\Bigr)
representing
d_{j}
in

\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T^{*}_{a})=\mathcal{U}_{k}\Bigl(H^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}_{k}}/K,T_{a}^{*})\Bigr).

By
[
4
, Lemma 3.9]
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find a Kolyvagin prime such that
\ell_{k}\in\mathcal{P}(T_{k})
such that

\displaystyle\textrm{loc}_{\ell_{k}}\Bigl((c_{i})_{1}^{(k)}\Bigr)\neq 0\Leftrightarrow\Bigl((c_{i})_{1}^{(k)}\Bigr)\neq 0\ \forall i=1,\ldots,s,

\displaystyle\textrm{loc}_{\ell_{k}}\Bigl(d_{j}^{(k)}\Bigr)\neq 0\Leftrightarrow d_{j}^{(k)}\neq 0\ \forall j=1,\ldots,t.

Let
\mathfrak{u}
be the ultraprime represented by the sequence
(\ell_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. By construction,
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
. Since
(c_{i})_{1}^{(k)}\neq 0
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
k
, because it represents a non-zero element in the ultraproduct, then
\textrm{loc}_{\ell_{k}}\Bigl((c_{i})_{1}^{(k)}\Bigr)\neq 0
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
k
. Hence
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}\bigl((c_{i})_{1}\bigr)
is a non-zero element in
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T_{1})
, so
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(c_{i})
generates
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)
for all
i
. Similarly, we can prove that
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(d_{j})
is non-zero for all
j
.
∎

The remainder of the section explains what can be achieved by choosing the right elements in Proposition
4.49
. First, we show that Assumption
(Proj)
implies that all cartesian Selmer structures contain elements that are not
\mathfrak{m}
-divisible.

Proposition 4.50
.

Suppose that
T
satisfies Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Proj)
. If
\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure defined on
T
and
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\neq 0
, there exists an element

c\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\setminus\mathfrak{m}\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/\Sigma,T).

Proof.

By Nakayama’s lemma, there exists a class

c\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\setminus\mathfrak{m}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T).

Then Proposition
2
implies that

c\notin\mathfrak{m}\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T),

where
\mathfrak{n}
is the square-free product of all the primes in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}_{s}}
.
∎

Combining the previous two results, we can obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4.51
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{s}
be cartesian structures, and let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{t}\in\textbf{H}^{1}(K,T^{*})\setminus\{0\}
, where
s+t<p
. Under Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Proj)
, if
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K,T)\neq 0
for all
i
, there exists an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
such that the maps

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(c_{i}):\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)

are surjective for all
i=1,\ldots,s
and
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(d_{j})\neq 0
for all
j=1,\ldots,t
.

We can also use Proposition
4.49
to make the dual Selmer group smaller.

Proposition 4.52
.

Assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure on
T
such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})\neq 0
. Then there exists
d\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})
such that, for any ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}
,

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(d)\neq 0\Rightarrow\dim_{R/\mathfrak{m}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})[\mathfrak{m}]<\dim_{R/\mathfrak{m}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})}(K,T^{*})[\mathfrak{m}].

Proof.

Nakayama’s lemma applied to the Pontryagin dual implies that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})\neq 0\Leftrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})[\mathfrak{m}]\neq 0.

Then the result follows by choosing a non-zero
m
-torsion element
d
.
∎

An inductive application of Proposition
4.52
implies the following theorem.

Corollary 4.53
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure. Under Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Proj)
, there is some
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
such that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,T^{*})=0.

When
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
is non-zero, we can construct a vertex
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
as above but also satisfying an additional property.

Corollary 4.54
.

Assume
p\geq 3
and let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure. Under Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Proj)
, if
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\neq 0
, there is some
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,T^{*})=0
and that, for every
\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{n}
, there is an equality

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*}).

Proof.

Choose a basis
\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{s}\}
of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,T^{*})[\mathfrak{m}]
as a
k
-vector space. By Corollary
4.51
, we can find ultraprimes
\mathfrak{u}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}_{i}}(r_{i})\neq 0
and the map

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}_{i}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}_{i}},T)

is surjective. For
\mathfrak{n}:=\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{s}
, we get
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,T^{*})=0
and, by Lemma
4.48
, we have that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u}_{i})}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}_{i}}}(K,T^{*}).\qed

When the core rank is positive, we can achieve an analogue of Corollary
4.53
with the transverse Selmer group.

Corollary 4.55
.

Assume that
p\geq 3
and let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure of core rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})=1
. Under Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Proj)
, there exists some
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T^{*})=0
.

Proof.

Similarly, let
\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{s}\}
be a basis of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,T^{*})[\mathfrak{m}]
as a
k
-vector space. Suppose inductively that we have constructed ultraprimes
\mathfrak{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}_{i}
such that
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}_{j}}(r_{j})\neq 0
for all
j\leq i
and

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{i})}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{i}}}(K,T^{*}).

Note that Proposition
4.47
implies that
\chi(\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{i}))=\chi(\mathcal{F})
is positive, so, in particular,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{i})}(K,T)\neq 0
. By Corollary
4.51
, we can find an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}}(r_{i+1})\neq 0
and the map

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{i})}(K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}},T)

is surjective. By Lemma
4.48
, we have that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{i+1})}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}}(\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{i})}(K,T^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}}}(K,T^{*}).

For
\mathfrak{n}:=\mathfrak{u}_{1}\ldots\mathfrak{u}_{s}
, we get
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T^{*})=0
.
∎

In addition to obtaining a trivial Selmer group by modifying the local condition at certain primes, we will also use a stronger result about reducing the rank of the Selmer group modifying the local condition at only one prime.

Proposition 4.56
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure such that

\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}>0.

Then there exists
d\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})
such that, for every ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}
, then

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(d)\neq 0\Rightarrow\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}=\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}-1.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.24
, there is an injection

\mu:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}_{\textrm{tors}}\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}.

Choose a
k
-basis
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}\}
of
\mu\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}_{\textrm{tors}}/\mathfrak{m}\Bigr)
and a set
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\}
extending it to a basis of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}/\mathfrak{m}
.

The localisation
(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}_{/\textrm{tors}})_{(0)}
is a finitely-generated
R_{(0)}
vector space of dimension
r
coinciding with the rank. Then any set of generators of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}_{/\textrm{tors}}
contains at least
r
elements, so Nakayama’s lemma implies that
t\geq r>0
.

Let
d\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})[\mathfrak{m}]
be the element of the dual basis associated with
g_{1}
, i.e., the unique element satisfying that
g_{1}(d)\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}
and
g_{i}(d)=0
for
i\geq 2
and
f_{i}(d)=0
for all
i
.

If
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
is a Kolyvagin ultraprime satisfying that
\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(d)\neq 0
. Consider then the following diagram:

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}})^{\vee}}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})^{\vee}\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}})^{\vee}}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}\otimes R/\mathfrak{m}.}

The image of the bottom horizontal map contains an element
h
satisfying that
h(d)\neq 0
. Hence the
{g_{1}}
-coordinate of
{d}
in the basis
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{s},g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\}
is non-zero. In particular, it implies that any lift of
h
in
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}
is not a torsion element.

Nevertheless, the image top horizontal arrow contains a lift of
h
, which will be a non-torsion element. In particular, the image has rank one. Since the rank is an additive function, we can conclude that

\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,T^{*})=\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})-1.\qed

Proposition 4.57
.

Suppose that
p\geq 3
and that
T
satisfies Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Proj)
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\neq 0
. Assume that

\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}>0.

Then there is an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
such that

\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}=\textrm{rank}_{R}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}-1.

Proof.

If is a direct application of Proposition
4.49
choosing the element in
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
given by Proposition
4.50
and the element in
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})
given by Proposition
4.56
.
∎

5
Iwasawa Selmer groups

From now on, we reduced the generality on the coefficient ring
R
and work with Selmer groups with coefficients in the Iwasawa algebra.

Assumption (Iw)
.

Let
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[X]]
be the classical Iwasawa algebra and let
T
be a
\Lambda[[G_{K}]]
-module that is free and finitely generated as a
\Lambda
-module. In addition, we assume the Galois action is only ramified at finitely many primes.

Notation 5.1
.

Following Remark
4.12
, we denote

\Lambda_{k}=\Lambda/(p^{k},X^{k}).

Note that the Iwasawa algebra satisfies Assumption
(Reg)
, so the results in the previous section also hold in this case.

Since the maximal ideal is not principal, we cannot use Remark
4.21
to prove Assumption
(Proj)
, which was required for the results in §
4.6
. However, we can show that Assumption
(Proj)
is equivalent to the following.

Assumption (H2)
.

There is a square-free product of ultraprimes
\mathfrak{n}
divisible by all the primes in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
such that the patched cohomology group
\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})
contains no finite
\Lambda
-submodules.

This assumption can be reinterpreted in terms of the local zero cohomology groups (see §
5.2
).

Proposition 5.2
.

Under Assumption
(BI)
, Assumptions
(Proj)
and
(H2)
are equivalent.

Proof.

Fix a square-free product of ultraprimes
\mathfrak{n}
divisible by all the primes in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
and consider the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
T
T
\textstyle{\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

By Remark
4.15
and Proposition
4.9
, it induces another exact sequence

\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T})\twoheadrightarrow\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})[X].

(6)

In addition, consider the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{T}/\mathfrak{m}\textbf{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Again, Proposition
4.9
and Remark
4.15
show that

\textbf{H}^{1}\Bigl(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}\Bigr)[p]=0

and that the following map is injective:

\textbf{H}^{1}\Bigl(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}\Bigr)\otimes\Lambda/(p)\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}\Bigl(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/\mathfrak{m}\textbf{T}\Bigr).

(7)

The snake lemma applied to the short exact sequence in (
6
) and the multiplication by
p
induces another exact sequence

\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})[\mathfrak{m}]\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/\mathfrak{m}\twoheadrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/(X))\otimes\Lambda/(p).

(8)

Combining (
7
) and (
8
), we obtain an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})[\mathfrak{m}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/\mathfrak{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/\mathfrak{m}\textbf{T}),}

that leads to the equivalence between Assumptions
(Proj)
and
(H2)
for the particular choice of
\mathfrak{n}
.
∎

Under Assumption
(BI)
, all the profinite Selmer groups are free modules.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure defined on a Galois representation
T
satisfying Assumptions
(BI)
. Then the Selmer group
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
is a free, finitely generated
\Lambda
-module.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.16
,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})
is a finitely generated, torsion free Iwasawa module, so the structure theorem (see
[
24
, Theorem 5.3.8]
) implies that it is pseudo-isomorphic to a free module. In addition, Proposition
4.16
implies that the Selmer group contains no finite submodules, so there is a free module
F
and a finite module
C
fitting in the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
C
is a non-trivial module. The snake lemma induces another exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F/XF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C/XC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

When
C
is non-trivial, then
C[X]\neq 0
, so
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)
contains a finite submodule.

Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\otimes\Lambda/(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}\Bigl(K,\textbf{T}/(X)\Bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,T)\otimes\Lambda/(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}\Bigl(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/(X)\Bigr),}

in which the left vertical map is injective by Proposition
2
and the bottom horizontal map is injective by the argument in Proposition
5.2
. It implies that

\textbf{H}^{1}\Bigl(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}\Bigr)[p]\neq 0,

contradicting Proposition
4.16
for the Galois representation
\textbf{T}/(X)
.
∎

We can use the Euler characteristic to compute the core rank of Iwasawa Selmer modules.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure defined on a Galois representation
T
satisfying Assumption
(BI)
. Denote

d_{-}(T):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\Sigma_{\infty}\\
v\ \textrm{real}\end{subarray}}\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{T}^{\sigma_{v}=-1}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\Sigma_{\infty}\\
v\ \textrm{complex}\end{subarray}}\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{T}.

where
\Sigma_{\infty}
denotes the archimedean places of
K
and
\sigma_{v}
denotes any complex conjugation. Then

\chi(\mathcal{F})=d_{-}(T)+\sum_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}\biggl(\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)-\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})^{\vee}\biggr).

(9)

Proof.

Combining Propositions
3.27
and
4.9
, there is an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/K,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}\textbf{H}^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

By Remark
4.15
,
H^{0}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/K,T)=0
. Then Corollary
3.21
and the standard limit argument imply that

d_{-}(T)=\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/K,T)-\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}}/K,T).

By Proposition
3.16
, there is an identification
\textbf{H}^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T^{*})^{\vee}
for every ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}
. Then the formula follows from the additivity of the rank.
∎

5.1
Core vertices

There are some special vertices in
\mathcal{N}
that control the Kolyvagin and Stark systems defined below. In particular, Stark systems are controlled by weak core vertices and Kolyvagin systems by core vertices.

Definition 5.5
.

A
weak core vertex
is a square-free product
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
of Kolyvagin ultraprimes such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})=0
.

Proposition 5.6
.

If
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
is a weak core vertex, then
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})
is a free module of rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.47
,
\chi(\mathcal{F}^{c})=\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(c)
, so the rank of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})
is
\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(c)
. By Proposition
5.3
,

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}.\qed

We can use Corollary
4.53
to prove the existence of a weak core vertex.

Proposition 5.7
.

Assume that
T
satisfies Assumption
(BI)
. Let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
be a square-free product of Kolyvagin ultraprimes. Then there exists a weak core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\mathfrak{n}\mid\mathfrak{c}
.

Proof.

It follows from a direct application of Corollary
4.53
to the Selmer structure
\mathcal{F}_{n}
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Note that the following converse result also holds: when
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
is a weak core vertex, every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
dividing
\mathfrak{c}
is also a weak core vertex.

As we will show below in §
7
, weak core vertices are used to control Stark systems. In order to have an analogue for Kolyvagin systems, we need a stronger version of core vertices.

Definition 5.9
.

A
core vertex
is a square-free product
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
of Kolyvagin ultraprimes such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})=0
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Suppose that
T
satisfies Assumption
(BI)
. If
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
is a core vertex,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T})
is a free module of rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.47
, then
\chi(\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{c}))=\chi(\mathcal{F})
, so the rank of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T})
is
\chi(\mathcal{F})
. By Proposition
5.3
, we have that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}.\qed

We can also show the existence of core vertices, using Corollary
4.55
.

Proposition 5.11
.

Consider a square-free product of Kolyvagin ultraprimes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
. If
T
satisfies Assumptions
(BI)
and
(H2)
, then there is a core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\mathfrak{n}\mid\mathfrak{c}
.

Proof.

It is an immediate consequence of Corollary
4.55
, which is applicable to this case due to Proposition
5.2
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

Proposition
5.11
is the only place where Assumption
(H2)
is needed. However, the existence of core vertices is a central step in the control of Kolyvagin systems, as will be shown in Theorem
8.7
below.

Remark 5.13
.

There is no analogue of Remark
5.8
for core vertices, since there might exist
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})
vanishes but
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})
does not.

5.2
Assumption on the second cohomology

In this section, we reinterpret Assumption
(H2)
on the lack finite
\Lambda
-submodules of
\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})
in terms of the local cohomology of bad primes.

Assumption (Tam)
.

For every
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
,
\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
contains no finite
\Lambda
-submodule.

Remark 5.14
.

By Proposition
3.16
, Assumption
(Tam)
is equivalent to the fact that
\textbf{H}^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})
does not containing any finite submodules for any
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Under Assumption
(BI)
, Assumption
(Tam)
implies Assumption
(H2)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be a weak core vertex for
\mathcal{F}
, which exists by Proposition
5.7
, and let
\Sigma
be the set consisting of the primes in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
and the prime divisors of
\mathfrak{n}
. By Proposition
3.27
, there is an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma}\textbf{H}^{1}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}(K^{\Sigma}/K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\Sigma}/K,\textbf{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma}\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}.}

Since
\mathfrak{n}
is a weak core vertex, the first map is surjective, so the proof of the lemma is reduced to showing that
\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
contains no finite non-trivial submodules for any
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma
. When
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
, this is an assumption, and when
\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{n}
, then
\mathfrak{u}
is a Kolyvagin prime, so

\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\cong\Lambda

also contains no finite non-zero submodules. Then Assumption
(H2)
holds by considering the square free product of primes in
\Sigma
.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

When
\ell
is a constant ultraprime, one condition implying that
\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\ell},\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
contains no-finite submodules is the
p
-divisibility of the dual group
\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\ell},\textbf{T}^{*})
.

Suppose that
T
comes from a
p
-adic representation
T
by Shapiro’s lemma, meaning that

\textbf{T}=T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\Gal(K_{\infty}/K)]],

where
K_{\infty}/K
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension at which no-finite prime splits completely. Then Shapiro’s lemma gives an isomorphism

\textbf{H}^{0}(K_{\ell},\textbf{T}^{*})=\bigoplus_{v\mid\ell}\textbf{H}^{0}\Bigl((K_{\infty})_{v},T^{*}\Bigr),

where the sum is taken over all the primes of
K_{\infty}
above
\ell
.

We assume the
p
-primality of the Tamagawa numbers associated with a Galois representation, which can be seen as the divisibility of the group
\textbf{H}^{0}\Bigl(K_{\ell}^{\textrm{ur}},T^{*}\Bigr)
.

Since
\ell
does not split completely at
K_{\infty}/K
, then
(K_{\infty})_{v}/K_{\ell}
is the unramified
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension of
K_{\ell}
. Hence the profinite degree
K_{\ell}^{\textrm{ur}}/(K_{\infty})_{v}
is prime to
p
, so its action on

\Aut\biggl(\textbf{H}^{0}\Bigl(K_{\ell}^{\textrm{ur}},T^{*}\Bigr)^{\vee}\biggr)\cong\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),

where
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is at most the rank of
T
, has finite image. Hence the eigenvalues of all the matrices in the image are roots of unity, implying that
\textbf{H}^{0}\Bigl((K_{\infty})_{v},T^{*}\Bigr)
is also a divisible group.

6
Algebraic preliminaries

In this section, we outline the algebraic preliminaries required for studying the structure of Iwasawa modules using Kolyvagin systems. The contents in this section include an introduction to the theory of Fitting ideals and the construction of the rank reduction map of exterior powers.

6.1
Fitting ideals

In this section, we define the Fitting ideals of modules and outline some results about their description of the structure of Iwasawa modules. For a more complete treatment, we refer the reader to
[
2
, §2.2]
and
[
6
, §2]
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
R
be a ring and let
M
be a finitely presented
R
-module. Choose a resolution

\textstyle{R^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\textstyle{R^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the map
R^{n}\to R^{m}
is represented by the matrix
A
. For every
i\geq 0
, we define the
i^{\textrm{th}}
Fitting ideal
\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{R}(M)
as the ideal of
R
generated by the minors of size
(m-i)
of
A
.

Remark 6.2
.

The
i^{\textrm{th}}
Fitting ideal coincides with the image of the following map, induced by the matrix
A
:

\textrm{Fitt}_{R}^{i}(M)=\textrm{Im}\left(\bigwedge^{m-i}R^{n}\to\bigwedge^{m-i}R^{m}\right).

It can be shown that Fitting ideals are well defined.

Proposition 6.3
.

(
[
7
, Corollary 20.4]
)
The Fitting ideals
\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{R}(M)
are independent of the chosen resolution.

The following results can be useful.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
R\to S
be a ring-homomorphism and let
M
be an
R
-module. Then

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{S}\Bigl(M\otimes_{R}S\Bigr)=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{R}(M)\otimes S\ \forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Proof.

Consider a resolution of
M
given by

\textstyle{R^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\textstyle{R^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since the tensor product is a right exact functor, we can obtain a resolution of
M\otimes S
:

\textstyle{S^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A\otimes S}
\textstyle{S^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\otimes_{R}S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Then the equality follows from the definition of Fitting ideals.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Assume that
R
is a domain and
M
is a torsion
R
-module of projective dimension at most one. Then
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{R}(M)
is a principal ideal.

Proof.

Since the projective dimension of
M
is at most one, it admits a resolution

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\textstyle{R^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since the rank is an additive function, then
m=n
, so
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}(M)
is generated by
\det(A)
.
∎

When
R
is the Iwasawa algebra
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[X]]
, the sequence Fitting ideals can recover the pseudo-isomorphism class of the module. Moreover, they see a finer structure, since they can be also used to study the pseudo-null submodules.

In order to determine the pseudo-isomorphism structure of a module, it is not necessary the complete knowledge of the Fitting ideals, only up to finite index.

Notation 6.6
.

When
M
is an Iwasawa module and
I
and
J
are two submodules, we say that
I
and
J
are pseudo-equal if, for every height one prime ideal
\mathcal{B}
, then
I_{\mathcal{B}}=J_{\mathcal{B}}
in
M_{\mathcal{B}}
. We then denote
I\approx J
. Note that this definition also applies to two ideals
I
and
J
of the Iwasawa algebra. If, in addition,
I\subset J
, we will write
I\subset_{f.i.}J
.

Proposition 6.7
.

(
[
6
, Theorem 2.8]
)
Let
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[X]]
be the Iwasawa algebra, and let
M
be a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module. Then the Fitting ideals determine
\Lambda
up to pseudo-isomorphism.

The pseudo-equality class of the
0^{\textrm{th}}
-Fitting ideal is additive in short exact sequences.

Corollary 6.8
.

(
[
6
, Theorem 2.8]
)
Let

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

be a short exact sequence of modules over a quotient of a discrete valuation ring
R
. Then there is a pseudo-equality

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(B)\approx\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(A)\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(C).

Fitting ideals can determine more than the structure of the module up to pseudo-isomorphism. In fact, they can determine whether a torsion Iwasawa module contains finite submodules.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
M
be a torsion Iwasawa module. Then
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(M)
is a principal ideal if and only if
M
contains no finite modules.

Proof.

If
M
contains no finite submodules,
[
24
, Proposition 5.5.3.(iv)]
implies that its projective dimension is at most one, so Proposition
6.5
implies that
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(M)
is principal.

Conversely, assume that
M
contains a finite submodule. Denote by
T_{0}(M)
the maximal finite submodule of
M
.

We can construct a projective resolution of
M
building from projective resolutions of
M
and
M/T_{0}(M)
, obtaining a diagram with exact rows and columns:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M/T_{0}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
A
B
C

Since
M/T_{0}(M)
contains no finite submodules,
[
24
, Proposition 5.5.3.(iv)]
implies that its projective dimension is at most one, which leads, under an appropriate choice of the map
C
, to the left exactness of the bottom row. Since it is a torsion module, we can assume that
c=z
, so
C
is a square matrix.

By the construction of the projective resolutions, the block representation of the matrix
B
is

N=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{array}[]{c;{2pt/2pt}c}A&*\\
\hline\cr[2pt/2pt]0_{x\times c}&C\end{array}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
y=x+z
, every non-zero minor of size
i
has to be a product of
\det(C)
with a minor of size
x
in
A
. Hence,

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(M)=\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(T_{0}(M)\Bigr)\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(M/T_{0}(M)\Bigr).

Note that
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(M/T_{0}(M)\Bigr)
is generated by
\det(C)\neq 0
since
C
is a square matrix and
M/T_{0}(M)
is a torsion module.

Hence the proof will be completed once we show that
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(T_{0}(M)\Bigr)
is a (non-principal) ideal strictly contained in
\Lambda
with finite index.

But every minor in
A
of size
x
cannot be a unit in
\Lambda
since, otherwise, the map generated by
A
would be surjective and
T_{0}(M)=0
, contradicting the assumption. Therefore,

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(T_{0}(M)\Bigr)\subset\mathfrak{m}.

Since
M_{\mathcal{B}}=0
for every height one prime ideal
\mathcal{B}
, we can conclude with Proposition
6.4
that

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(T_{0}(M)\Bigr)_{\mathcal{B}}=\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}}\Bigl(T_{0}(M)_{\mathcal{B}}\Bigr)=\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}},

so
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(T_{0}(M)\Bigr)
has finite index in
\Lambda
.
∎

6.2
Rank reduction of exterior powers

The goal of this section is to introduce a rank reduction map between exterior powers of Iwasawa modules. It will be based on the construction of
[
2
, §2]
.

Definition 6.10
.

The
exterior bidual
of rank
r
of a module
M
is defined as

\bigcap^{r}_{\Lambda}M=\Hom\left(\bigwedge^{r}M^{*},R\right),

where
\displaystyle\bigwedge^{r}M^{*}
denotes the exterior power of
M
.

Proposition 6.11
.

(
[
2
, Lemma 2.17.]
)
Let
s
be a natural number and consider the exact sequence of
\Lambda
-modules

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Lambda^{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

For every
r\geq s
is a natural number, there is a canonical map

\phi_{N,M}:\ \bigcap^{r}N\to\bigcap^{r-s}M.

Proof.

It follows from
[
2
, Lemma 2.17.]
, since the Iwasawa algebra is a Gorenstein ring by
[
2
, Lemma 2.3]
.
∎

Although this construction is more natural using biduals, it can be expressed as a rank reduction of exterior powers when
M
and
N
are free modules.

Corollary 6.12
.

In the notation of Proposition
6.11
, assume in addition that
M
and
N
are free modules. Then there is a canonical rank reduction map

\phi_{N,M}:\ \bigwedge^{r}N\to\bigwedge^{r-s}M

Proof.

It follows from the canonical isomorphism

\bigcap^{r}_{\Lambda}F\cong\bigwedge^{r}_{\Lambda}F

for any free Iwasawa module.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

The map in Corollary
6.12
can be constructed directly. See
[
5
, §2.1]
for details.

The rank reduction map can be used to compute Fitting ideals.

Definition 6.14
.

Let
M
be an Iwasawa module module and let
a\in M
. Consider the canonical map into the bidual module

\Phi:\ M\to M^{++}:a\in M\mapsto\Bigl[\varphi\in\Hom(M,R)\mapsto\varphi(a)\Bigr].

The
index
of
a
is defined as

\ind(a,M):=\textrm{Im}(\Phi(a)).

Proposition 6.15
.

Assume, in the notation of Proposition
6.11
, that
N
is also a torsion-free module of rank
r+s
and let
\psi
be a generator in
\bigcap^{r+s}N
. Then

\ind(\phi_{N,M})\approx\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(C).

Remark 6.16
.

The structure theorem implies that
N
is pseudo-isomorphic to
\Lambda^{r}
. Dualizing, since
\Ext^{1}(C,\Lambda)=0
for all finite Iwasawa modules
C
(see
[
24
, Corollary 5.5.9]
), we get that
N^{+}\cong\Lambda^{r}
, so

\bigcap^{r}N\cong\bigwedge^{r}\Lambda^{r}=\Lambda.

Hence there exists a generator
\psi
of
\bigcap^{r+s}N
.

Proof of Proposition
6.15
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a height one prime ideal of
\Lambda
. Note that
\ind\Bigl(\Phi_{N,M}(\psi)\Bigr)_{\mathcal{B}}
coincides with the image of the composition

\textstyle{\bigwedge^{r-s}N_{\mathcal{B}}^{+}\otimes\det(F^{+})_{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigwedge^{r-s}M_{\mathcal{B}}^{+}\otimes\det(F^{+})_{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigwedge^{r}N_{\mathcal{B}}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \ \ \ \psi}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}.}

After localising with a height
1
prime ideal
\mathcal{B}
of
\Lambda
and fixing an isomorphism
\det(F)_{\mathcal{B}}\cong\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}
, Proposition
6.4
and Remark
6.2
imply that

\textrm{Fitt}_{\Lambda}^{0}(C)_{\mathcal{B}}=\textrm{Im}\left(\bigwedge^{s}N_{\mathcal{B}}\to\det(F)_{\mathcal{B}}\right).

Note that
N_{\mathcal{B}}
is a free
\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}
-module of rank
r
and consider the image of the dual map

I:=\textrm{Im}\left(\det(F^{+})_{\mathcal{B}}\to\bigwedge^{s}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}\right).

Since
\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}
is a discrete valuation ring, every generator
\alpha
of
I
has index
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{R}(C)_{\mathcal{B}}
, so there is an element
\beta\in\bigwedge^{s}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}
and a generator
\gamma
of
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{R}(C)_{\mathcal{B}}
such that
\alpha=\gamma\beta
and
\beta
projects to a non-zero element

\overline{\beta}\in\bigwedge^{s}(N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}/\mathcal{B}).

That implies that there exists a linearly independent set in the
\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}/\mathcal{B}\Lambda_{\mathcal{B}}
vector space
N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}/\mathcal{B}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}
,
S=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}\}
, such that

\overline{\beta}=e_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{s}.

Consider a set
\{e_{s+1},\ldots,e_{r}\}
extending
S
to a basis of
N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}/\mathcal{B}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}
. Then,

\alpha\wedge e_{s+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{r}\in\bigwedge^{r}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}

generates the submodule
\left(\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{R}(C)_{\mathcal{B}}\right)\cdot\bigwedge^{r}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}
. In addition, every element in

I\otimes\bigwedge^{r-s}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}\subset\bigwedge^{s}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}

is
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{R}(C)_{\mathcal{B}}
-divisible in the last exterior power. Hence,

I\otimes\bigwedge^{r-s}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}=\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{R}(C)\bigwedge^{s}N^{+}_{\mathcal{B}}.

Using the comment at the beginning of this proof, we get that

\ind\Bigl(\Phi_{N,M}(\psi)\Bigr)_{\mathcal{B}}=\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{R}(C)_{\mathcal{B}}.\qed

There is one special case in which we can prove that the index is contained in the Fitting ideal. That is because we can show that this index is a principal ideal, so it contains all ideals to which it is pseudo-equal (see Notation
6.6
).

Proposition 6.17
.

Assume we have an exact sequence of finitely generated Iwasawa modules over the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda
,

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
M
,
N
and
F
are torsion-free Iwasawa modules of ranks

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}N=r,&\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}F=s,\end{array}

for some natural numbers
r\geq s
. Let
\psi
be a generator of
\bigcap^{r}N
. Then the index
\ind\Bigl(\Phi_{N,M}(\psi)\Bigr)
is the unique Fitting ideal containing
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(C)
with finite index.

Proof.

Since
M
is torsion-free, it is pseudo-isomorphic to a free Iwasawa module. Assume that its rank is larger than
r-s
. The additivity of the rank implies that
C
has positive rank, so

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(C)=0.

Since
\Lambda
contains no finite ideals, Proposition
6.15
implies that

\ind(\Phi_{N,M})(\psi)=0.

Now assume that the rank of
M
is
r-s
. Then
M
is pseudo-isomorphic to
\Lambda^{r-s}
. As in Remark
6.16
, we can conclude that

\bigcap^{r-s}M\cong\bigcap^{r-s}\Lambda^{r-s}=\Lambda.

Then the index of every element in
\bigcap^{r-s}M
is a principal ideal and, in particular, the index of
\phi_{N,M}(\psi)
. Since the principal ideals of the Iwasawa algebra contain all the ideals they are pseudo-equal to, we can conclude by Proposition
6.15
that

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(C)\subset\ind\Bigl(\Phi_{N,M}(\psi)\Bigr).\qed

The rank reduction maps satisfy the following commutative property. The following proposition follows from
[
30
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Proposition 6.18
.

(
[
30
, Proposition 2.4]
)
Consider the following exact sequences of Iwasawa algebra,

173.89243pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 173.89243pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
