satisfying that

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu_{13}=\mu_{23}\circ\mu_{12},&\pi_{23}=(\pi_{13})_{2},&\pi_{12}=(\pi_{13})_{1}\circ\mu_{23},\end{array}

(10)

where
(\pi_{13})_{i}
for
i=1,2
denotes the composition of
\pi_{13}
projection to the first or second component, respectively, in
\Lambda^{s+s^{\prime}}=\Lambda^{s}\oplus\Lambda^{s^{\prime}}
. Then

\phi_{M_{3},M_{1}}=\phi_{M_{3},M_{2}}\circ\phi_{M_{2},M_{1}}.

It is possible to compute the kernel of the rank reduction maps.

Proposition 6.19
.

(
[
2
, Lemma 2.17]
)
Let
M
and
N
be modules over a ring
R
which is either self-injective, a discrete valuation ring or the Iwasawa algebra. In addition, let

\phi_{N,M}:\ \bigcap^{r}N\to\bigcap^{r-s}M

be the rank reduction map constructed in Proposition
6.11
for the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{R,}

where
F
is a free
\Lambda
module of rank
s
. Consider also the map

\iota:\ \bigcap^{r}M\hookrightarrow\bigcap^{r}N.

Then
\ker(\phi_{N,M})=\textrm{Im}(\iota)
.

Remark 6.20
.

All the results in this section can be applied to the map constructed in Corollary
6.12
, provided that
M
and
N
are free Iwasawa modules.

7
Stark Systems

The goal of this section is to give a construction of the ultra Stark systems, analogous to the classical case in
[
30
]
and
[
5
]
. In order to do that, we assume that we are given a cartesian Selmer structure defined on a Galois representation satisfying Assumption
(BI)
.

Let
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathcal{N}
be square-free products of Kolyvagin ultraprimes such that
\mathfrak{n}\mid\mathfrak{r}
. There is an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{r}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}).}

We want to use Corollary
6.12
to construct a map

\phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}:\ \bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{r})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{r}}}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{n})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T}).

In order to do that, we fix, for every Kolyvagin ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
, an isomorphism

\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda.

In addition, we need to fix an order in the set
\mathcal{P}
. Such ordering fixes an isomorphism

\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda^{\nu(\mathfrak{r})-\nu(\mathfrak{n})}.

Then the map
\phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}
is the one given by Corollary
6.12
.

In order to guarantee the commutative property of these maps, we need to modify the sign convention. Following
[
5
, §3.1]
, we make the following definition.

Definition 7.1
.

For every pair of elements
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\mathfrak{n}\mid\mathfrak{r}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}_{\nu(\mathfrak{n})}
and
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}_{\nu(\mathfrak{r}/\mathfrak{n})}
the prime divisors of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{r}/\mathfrak{n}
, ordered by the predetermined ordering. Similarly, let
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}^{\prime\prime}_{\nu(\mathfrak{r})}
the ordered set of prime divisors of
\mathfrak{r}
. Define
\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n})
as the sign of the permutation

(\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}_{\nu(\mathfrak{r}/\mathfrak{n})},\mathfrak{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}_{\nu(\mathfrak{n})})\mapsto(\mathfrak{u}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}^{\prime\prime}_{\nu(\mathfrak{r})}).

Definition 7.2
.

We define the map

\Phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}=\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n})\cdot\phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}:\ \bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{r})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{r}}}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{n})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T}),

where
\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n})
is defined in Definition
7.1
and the map
\phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}
is the map given in Corollary
6.12
. Note that this map is well defined only because both Selmer groups are free (see Proposition
5.3
).

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{s}\in\mathcal{N}
be square-free products of Kolyvagin ultraprimes such that
\mathfrak{n}\mid\mathfrak{r}\mid\mathfrak{s}
. Then

\Phi_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{n}}=\Phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}}.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.18
, the map

\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r})\cdot\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n})\cdot\bigl(\Phi_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}\bigr)

is the map induced from Corollary
6.12
by the exact sequence

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{s}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\times\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{r}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}}

and the isomorphisms

\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda^{\nu(\mathfrak{r}/\mathfrak{n})}},&\displaystyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{r}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda^{\nu(\mathfrak{s}/\mathfrak{r})},}\end{array}

induced by the order choice.

Similarly, the map
\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{n})\Phi_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{n}}
is induced by the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{s}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}}

and the isomorphism

\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)\cong\Lambda^{\nu(\mathfrak{s}/\mathfrak{n})}.

Consider the following diagram in which the vertical isomorphisms are induced by the ones above mentioned and the horizontal ones are the natural maps:

\textstyle{\displaystyle{\det\left(\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\times\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{r}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\right)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\det\left(\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\right)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\det\left(R^{\nu(\mathfrak{r}/\mathfrak{n})}\times R^{\nu(\mathfrak{s}/\mathfrak{r})}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\det\left(R^{\nu(\mathfrak{s}/\mathfrak{n})}\right).}

Then this diagram is commutative up to the sign

\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r})\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{n})\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}).

By the identifications made above, we get that

\Phi_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{n}}=\Phi_{\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}.\qed

Therefore, the set of maps
\Phi_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}}
forms an inverse system, so it makes sense to consider the elements in the inverse limit.

Definition 7.4
.

The module of Stark systems of
\mathcal{F}
is defined as the inverse limit

\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}):=\varprojlim_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(n)}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})\right).

Remark 7.5
.

Unlike the construction in
[
4
]
, we opted to construct Stark systems as collections in the exterior powers of Selmer groups instead of exterior biduals. The reason for this choice is that Iwasawa Selmer groups are free modules, so the rank reduction maps are well defined on exterior powers. It is then unnecessary to consider the exterior biduals, although it will be useful in §
9
to compare the ultra Kolyvagin systems with the classical theory.

The link between Stark systems and the Fitting ideals of Selmer groups involves the construction of a sequence of theta ideals.

Definition 7.6
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be a Stark system. We define its theta ideals as

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\varepsilon):=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\ind\left(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}},\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{n})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})\right).

7.1
The module of Stark systems

The main goal of this section will be to prove the following theorem, which says that Stark systems are controlled by weak core vertices.

Theorem 7.7
.

Suppose that
T
satisfies Assumption
(BI)
and let
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
be a weak core vertex. Then the projection map

\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.7
, we only need to prove that if
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
is a weak core vertex and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
does not divide
\mathfrak{c}
, the map

\Phi_{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{c}}:\ \bigwedge^{r+\nu(\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{u})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{u}}}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{r+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})

is an isomorphism. This map is induced by the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{u}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Then Proposition
6.15
implies that
\Phi_{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{c}}
is surjective so, being both domain and codomain free modules of rank one, it is an isomorphism.
∎

The above control theorem allows the computation of the module of Stark systems.

Corollary 7.8
.

If
T
satisfies Assumption
(BI)
, the module of Stark systems
\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
is a free
\Lambda
-module of rank one.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
be a weak core vertex. By Proposition
5.10
,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})
is free of rank
{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}
. Thus, Theorem
7.7
implies that

\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\cong\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\cong\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\Lambda^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}=\Lambda.\qed

Therefore, it makes sense to talk about generators of the module of Stark systems.

Definition 7.9
.

The generators of
\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
are called
primitive
Stark systems.

Theorem 7.10
.

Suppose that Assumption
(BI)
holds and let
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}\in\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be a primitive Stark system. For every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, we have that
\ind(\varepsilon_{n})
is a principal ideal such that

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset_{f.i.}\ind(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}).

Proof.

Fix
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
. By Remark
5.8
, there exists a core vertex
\mathfrak{c}
such that
\mathfrak{n}\mid\mathfrak{c}
. Consider then the map in Definition
7.2
:

\Phi_{\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{n}}:\ \bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{n})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T}).

Since
\varepsilon
generates
\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
, Theorem
7.7
implies that
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}
generates

\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T}).

Since
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}=\Phi_{\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{n}}(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{c}})
, then Proposition
6.17
implies that

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset_{f.i.}\ind(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}).\qed

7.2
Higher Fitting ideals of the Selmer group

We can also use Stark systems to compute higher Fitting ideals of the Selmer group. Indeed, we will see the higher Fitting ideals coincide up to finite index with the theta ideals defined in Definition
7.6

Theorem 7.11
.

If Assumption
(BI)
holds, every primitive Stark system
\varepsilon\in\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
satisfies that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset_{f.i.}\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\varepsilon).

Proof.

Choose a weak core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
, which induces an exact sequence

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{c}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}.}

For every
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{c}
, there is another exact sequence

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{q}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{q}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{q}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}.}

By the definition of the Fitting ideals, we can deduce that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{c}}\textrm{Fitt}^{i-1}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{q}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

Proposition
5.7
implies that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}}\textrm{Fitt}^{i-1}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{q}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

We can now proceed to prove Theorem
7.11
by induction. For
i=0
, it is Theorem
7.10
for
n=1
. Assuming the result holds true for
i-1
, we can apply it to all Selmer structures
\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{q}}
to obtain that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{i-1},\ \mathfrak{q}\nmid\mathfrak{n}}\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

Rearranging the sum,

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

By Theorem
7.10
, we obtain that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\lessapprox\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\ind(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}})=\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\varepsilon).\qed

Remark 7.12
.

It is expected that the ideals
\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\varepsilon)
can be recovered as a limit of the analogues of classical Stark systems, as those appearing in
[
4
]
. If that comparison can be done, Theorem
7.11
can be seen as a weaker version of
[
4
, Theorem 4.12]
.

8
Kolyvagin systems

In this section, we develop the theory of ultra Kolyvagin systems in order to give a computation of the Fitting ideals of Selmer groups. Although that has already been done using Stark systems, the computations of the theta ideals associated with Kolyvagin systems are more explicit from the classes in the Euler system.

8.1
Definition of Kolyvagin systems

Unlike Stark systems, Kolyvagin systems can be only defined when the core rank is positive. The reason is that Kolyvagin system relations will be defined on an exterior power of rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})-1
, which is only constructed when the core rank is positive. In addition, we need to assume that the prime
p
is at least
5
. This assumption was already made in
[
21
]
and it is required so we can construct suitable primes using Proposition
4.49
.

Assumption (Pos)
.

\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure of core rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})\geq 1
and the prime
p
is at least
5
.

For every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
, there are exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})_{\mathfrak{u}}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}),}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})_{\mathfrak{u}}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}).}

Recall that, in the previous section, we had defined an isomorphism

\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\to R.

Together with the finite-singular map defined in Proposition
4.44
, it induces another isomorphism

\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\circ\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}^{\textrm{fs}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\cong R.

Under these identifications, the above exact sequences and Corollary
6.12
induce maps

\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}:\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,\textbf{T}),

\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}:\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,\textbf{T}).

Definition 8.1
.

A
Kolyvagin system
is a collection

(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}\in\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\right)

satisfying, for all
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
not dividing
\mathfrak{n}
, that

\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})=\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}).

Remark 8.2
.

When
\chi(\mathcal{F})=1
, Definition
8.1
coincides with the construction in
[
21
]
, modified as in
[
32
]
to consider local conditions at non-constant ultraprimes. Indeed, one could see that

\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})=\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})),&\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}})=\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}(\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}})).\end{array}

Only because
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
are defined satisfying
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\circ\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}^{\textrm{fs}}
, we can see that Kolyvagin system relations in both settings are equivalent.

Remark 8.3
.

By Proposition
5.3
, all the Selmer groups
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})
are free modules, so there is a canonical identification

\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})=\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}).

Similarly to Definition
7.6
, we can associate a sequence of theta ideals with every Kolyvagin system.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be a Kolyvagin systems. We define its theta ideals as

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa):=\sum_{n\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\ind\left(\kappa_{n},\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\right).

8.2
Kolyvagin systems from Stark systems

In this section, we define a regulator map from the module of Stark systems to the Kolyvagin systems and prove that it is, indeed, an isomorphism.

For every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, there is an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{n}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid n}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}).}

With the ordering of the ultraprimes previously established, there is a well defined isomorphism

\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid n}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \prod_{\mathfrak{u}\mid n}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda^{\nu(n)}.

With these identifications, Corollary
6.12
constructs a map

(-1)^{\nu(\mathfrak{n})}\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}:\ \bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(n)}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,T)\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T).

The sign modification is introduced to guarantee that the regulator maps produce Kolyvagin systems.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure of core rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})\geq 1
. Combined for all
n\in\mathcal{N}
, the maps
\textrm{Reg}_{n}
induce a regulator map

\textrm{Reg}:\ \textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

We need to show that the image of the regulator of a Stark system satisfies the Kolyvagin system relations, i.e., that for every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
not dividing
\mathfrak{n}
, we have that

\Bigl(\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}}\Bigr)(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}})=\Bigl(\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}\Bigr)(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}),

since the Stark system relation implies that
\Phi_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}}(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}})=\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}
.

The maps
\Bigl(\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}}\Bigr)
and
\Bigl(\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}\Bigr)
can be computed using Corollary
6.12
and the exact sequences

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.3924pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.3924pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.3924pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.3924pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 55.3924pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 135.26053pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 135.26053pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\displaystyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\oplus\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{n}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{q}},\textbf{T})}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\oplus\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}),

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{q}},\textbf{T})\oplus\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}.}

Note that the ordering matters to establish an isomorphism to
\Lambda^{\nu(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})+1}
, used in Corollary
6.12
to construct the rank reduction maps
3
3

3

A different order of the summands might result in a sign difference when constructing the rank reduction maps.
. It is important to clarify that, when we write
{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{a}}}
, we are using the canonical order in the ultraprimes dividing
\mathfrak{a}
.

With these ordering choices, the maps constructed by Corollary
6.12
differ from
\Bigl(\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}}\Bigr)
and
\Bigl(\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}\Bigr)
in the signs of
(-1)^{\nu(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}\textrm{sign}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n})
and
(-1)^{\nu(\mathfrak{n})}
, respectively. This sign difference is due to a reordering of the summands, so we can conclude that

\Bigl(\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}}\Bigr)=\Bigl(\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\circ\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}\Bigr).\qed

It is especially important to note that the regulator maps are isomorphisms on core vertices.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
be a core vertex. Then the regulator map

\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}:\ \bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(n)}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.10
,

\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(n)}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})\cong\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda

and, by Proposition
6.15
, the regulator of a generator of
\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(n)}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T})
is an indivisible element in
\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})
, so
\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}
is an isomorphism.
∎

8.3
The module of Kolyvagin systems

We will see in this section that the module of Kolyvagin systems is controlled by core vertices, similarly to the control made by weak core vertices to Stark systems in Theorem
7.7
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Suppose that Assumptions
(Pos)
,
(BI)
and
(H2)
hold and let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
be a core vertex. Then the projection map

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(n)}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})

is an isomorphism.

The proof of Theorem
8.7
is more complicated than its analogous in Theorem
7.7
. It will be presented below as a sequence of lemmas. We start by showing the surjectivity.

Lemma 8.8
.

Under assumptions
(Pos)
,
(BI)
and
(H2)
, the projection map

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T})

is surjective for every core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Reg
\textstyle{\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\bigwedge^{r+\nu(c)}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{c}}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\bigwedge^{r}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T}).}

By Theorem
7.7
and Proposition
8.6
, the left vertical map and the bottom horizontal map are isomorphisms. Hence, the right vertical map is surjective.
∎

We next prove the injectivity of the map in Theorem
8.7
. The argument takes a Kolyvagin system in the kernel of this map and proves inductively that all the classes
\kappa_{n}
have to vanish. The following set of vertices, containing all core vertices, have control over the Kolyvagin systems.

Definition 8.9
.

Let
\mathcal{N}^{(0)}\subset\mathcal{N}
be the subset of vertices defined as

\mathcal{N}^{(0)}:=\left\{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}:\ \textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}=0\right\}.

Notation 8.10
.

For every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, we define the module
\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})
as the unique cyclic submodule of
\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})
such that

\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})\approx\biggl(\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\biggr)\cdot\left(\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\right).

Note that
\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})=0
whenever
\mathfrak{n}\notin\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
.

Notation 8.11
.

In addition, we denote
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}
the unique cyclic submodule of
\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})
as the unique cyclic submodule satisfying that

\Omega_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\approx\biggl(\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\biggr)\cdot\left(\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\right).

Remark 8.12
.

Both
\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})
and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}
are well-defined since, whenever the Fitting ideal in the product is non-zero, the exterior power is a free Iwasawa module of rank one.

Lemma 8.13
.

Suppose that Assumptions
(Pos)
,
(BI)
and
(H2)
hold and let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
be elements such that
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
. Then there is an isomorphism

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}:\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})\to\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})

such that every
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
satisfying that
\kappa_{n}\in\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})
, then
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{n})=\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}
.

Proof.

Assume first that the rank of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
is zero or, equivalently, that

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\not\approx 0.

Denote

C_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}:=\textrm{coker}\biggl(\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{q}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{q}},\textbf{T})\biggr).

By Proposition
4.9
and Corollary
6.8

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\cong\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(C_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}})\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

(11)

Since
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
,
\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})
is free of rank one, so Proposition
6.15
implies that the map
\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}
induces an isomorphism

\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}:\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}\cdot\left(\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\right),

where
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}
denotes the unique principal ideal of
\Lambda
pseudo-equal to
\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}(C_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}})
. By (
11
), it restricts to an isomorphism

\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}:\ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})\to\Omega_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}.

A similar argument leads to another isomorphism

\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}:\ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})\to\Omega_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}.

We can thus define the isomorphism

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}:=\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}:\ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})\to\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}).

Assume now that the rank of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
is one. By the argument in Corollary
4.57
, we can find an ultraprime
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
such that the rank of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
is zero and
C_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}=0
. Then the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textrm{coker}(\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

given by Proposition
4.9
, implies that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})
has rank
0
, since
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, so we can define the map
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{q}}
as above.

On the other hand, since the rank of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
is one, the fact that
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
and Proposition
4.9
imply that the following maps are zero:

\displaystyle\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}),

\displaystyle\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}).

Hence
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T})
. Then the map

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{q}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{q}},\textbf{T})

is also surjective. Similarly, since
\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
has rank
0
. Hence the map
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{q}}
can be defined.

Finally, the map

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{q}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{q}},\textbf{T})

is surjective too. Thus Lemma
4.48
implies that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{q})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*}).

By the assumption on the ultraprime
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
, we have that the Selmer group
\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{q})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
has rank zero, implying that
\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{q}
are both in
\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
and that the map
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{q}}
can be defined. We then define the map

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{q}}\circ\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{q}}:\ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})\to\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}).

By construction, every Kolyvagin system
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
in which
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n})
satisfies that
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})=\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}}
.
∎

Remark 8.14
.

One can show that the map
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}
is independent of the choice of the auxiliary prime
\mathfrak{q}
. However, we do not make use of this fact and only need the existence of such map for our purposes.

Lemma 8.15
.

Suppose that
T
satisfies Assumptions
(Pos)
,
(BI)
and
(H2)
. Let
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}
and let
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
be vertices such that
\mathfrak{q}_{1}\mid\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{2}\mid\mathfrak{n}_{2}
. Then there is an ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
such that both
\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
for
i=1,2
and there are isomorphisms

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}}:\ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{i})\to\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})

such that, for all
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
, if
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}}\in\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{i})
, the equality
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})=\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}}
holds.

Proof.

We can apply Propositions
4.49
and
4.56
to find
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{P}
such that for
i=1,2
,

•

If
\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T})\geq 1
, the map

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})

is surjective.

•

If
\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T})=0
, the map

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})

(12)

is surjective.

•

\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}=0
.

Note that the last condition can be achieved since
\mathfrak{n}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
implies that
\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\leq 1

Independently of the rank of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{(}\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}}(K,\textbf{T})
, the map in (
12
) is also surjective, so Lemma
4.48
implies that

\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}=\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}=0,

so
\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
.

When
\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}}(K,\textbf{T})\geq 1
, the map

\textrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{u}}:\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})

is surjective, so Lemma
4.48
implies that

\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})=\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n}_{i})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})=0,

where the last equality follows from the fact
\mathfrak{n}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
.

In this case, we can construct the map
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}}
as the composition of maps constructed in Lemma
8.15
, i.e.,

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}}.

When
\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}}(K,\textbf{T})=0
, then we necessarily have, by Proposition
4.47
, that
\chi(\mathcal{F})=1
and, by Proposition
4.9
, we also get that

\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})^{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}=\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i})}(K,T^{*})^{\vee}=0.

We can now use the maps in Lemma
8.13
, to construct

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}\mathfrak{u}/\mathfrak{q}_{i}}\circ\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{i}/\mathfrak{q}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i}}^{-1}.\qed

Remark 8.16
.

Lemma
8.15
is the only place in this proof where the condition
p\geq 5
is required. For the other parts of this proof, it is enough to assume that
p\geq 3
.

We can now use the previous two lemmas to construct the map to construct such maps for any two core vertices.

Lemma 8.17
.

Under Assumptions
(Pos)
,
(BI)
and
(H2)
, there is, for every
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
, an isomorphism

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}}:\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{1})\to\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{2})

such that, for every
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
satisfying that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}\in\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{1})
, then
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}})=\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}
.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that the quantity
s=\nu(\mathfrak{n}_{2})-\nu(\mathfrak{n}_{1})\geq 0
. A recursive application of Corollary
4.54
shows the existence of primes
\mathfrak{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{u}_{s}
such that for all
i
, the product
\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
. Let
\mathfrak{r}:=\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}_{1}\cdots\mathfrak{u}_{s}
. By Lemma
8.13
, we can construct by composition a map

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{r}}:\ \Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{1})\to\Upsilon(\mathfrak{r}),

such that every Kolyvagin system
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
such that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}\in\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{1})
satisfies that
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{r}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}})=\kappa_{\mathfrak{r}}
.

For every prime
\mathfrak{q}_{1}\mid\mathfrak{r}
not dividing
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
and every prime
\mathfrak{q}_{2}\mid\mathfrak{n}_{2}
and not dividing
\mathfrak{r}
, we can apply Lemma
8.15
recursively to find the map

\gamma_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}_{2}}:\Upsilon(\mathfrak{r})\to\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{2}),

such that every Kolyvagin system
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
such that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{r}}\in\Upsilon(\mathfrak{r})
satisfies that
\gamma_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}_{1}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{r}})=\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}
.
The isomorphism
\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}}
is constructed as the composition of these maps:

\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n}_{2}}\circ\gamma_{\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{r}}.

∎

Since, for every core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
, we have that

\Upsilon(\mathfrak{c})=\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}),

the following corollary holds.

Corollary 8.18
.

Let
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be a Kolyvagin system. If
\kappa_{\mathfrak{c}}=0
for some core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
, then
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}=0
for any other vertices
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)}
. In particular,
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}=0
for all other core vertices
\mathfrak{n}
.

Remark 8.19
.

If we apply Lemma
8.17
with
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
being a core vertex, we obtain that

\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}\in\Upsilon(\mathfrak{n}_{2})\ \forall\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}^{(0)},\ \forall\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}).

Lemma 8.20
.

Let
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be a Kolyvagin system such that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{c}}=0
for some core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
. Then
\kappa=0
.

Proof.

We will show that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}=0
for all
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
. We will proceed by induction on the quantity

\lambda(\mathfrak{n})=\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\ \textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}.

When
\lambda(\mathfrak{n})=0
, then we can deduce by Lemma
8.17
that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}=0
.

Assume now that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{r}}=0
for all
\mathfrak{r}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\lambda(\mathfrak{r})\leq k
for some natural number
k
and that
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
is a vertex satisfying that
\lambda(\mathfrak{n})=k
and that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}\neq 0
.

By Corollary
4.57
and Proposition
6.19
, we can construct a Kolyvagin ultraprime such that

\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}\notin\ker\left(\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\right)

and
\lambda(\mathfrak{n}\tau)<\lambda(\mathfrak{n})
. By the induction hypothesis,
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\tau}=0
, which contradicts the Kolyvagin relation between
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\tau}
. The contradiction proves that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}=0
for all
\kappa_{n}\in\mathcal{N}
.
∎

We can now conclude the proof of Theorem
8.7
.

Proof of Theorem
8.7
.

The projection

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})

is surjective by Lemma
8.8
and injective by Lemma
8.20
.
∎

Hence we know that the module of Kolyvagin systems is a free, cyclic
\Lambda
-module that is controlled by the module of Stark systems.

Corollary 8.21
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure of core rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})\geq 1
. If
T
satisfies Assumptions
(BI)
and
(H2)
, the set of Kolyvagin systems is a free, cyclic
\Lambda
-module.

Corollary 8.22
.

Under Assumptions
(Pos)
,
(BI)
and
(H2)
, the regulator map

\textrm{Reg}:\ \textbf{SS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})

is an isomorphism.

It now makes sense to consider generators of the module of Kolyvagin systems.

Definition 8.23
.

We say that a Kolyvagin system
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
is
primitive
if it generates
\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
as a
\Lambda
-module.

Analogously to Theorem
7.10
, primitive Kolyvagin systems control the Fitting ideal with transverse local conditions.

Theorem 8.24
.

Suppose Assumptions
(BI)
and
(H2)
hold and that
\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure of core rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})\geq 1
. If
p\geq 5
and
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
is a primitive Kolyvagin system, then

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset_{f.i.}\ind(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})\ \forall\,\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}.

Proof.

By Corollary
8.22
, there is a primitive Stark system
\varepsilon
such that
\textrm{Reg}(\varepsilon)=\kappa
. For every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, Proposition
5.7
shows the existence of a weak core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\mathfrak{n}\mid\mathfrak{c}
. Then Proposition
4.9
gives an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{n}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\oplus\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{n}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Then
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}=(\textrm{Reg}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ\phi_{\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{n}})(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{c}})
. Since
\varepsilon
is a primitive Stark system, Theorem
7.7
shows that
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}
generates
\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})+\nu(\mathfrak{c})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{c}}(K,\textbf{T})
. Hence Propositions
6.15
and
6.17
imply that

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset_{f.i.}\ind(\kappa_{n}).\qed

8.4
Fitting ideals of the Selmer group

In this section, we give a description of the Fitting ideals of the Selmer group, by relating them with the theta ideals of a primitive Kolyvagin system.

Theorem 8.25
.

Let
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be a primitive Kolyvagin system and suppose that Assumptions
(Pos)
,
(BI)
and
(H2)
hold. Then

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset_{f.i.}\mathbf{\Theta}(\kappa).

Proof.

By Corollary
4.54
, we can choose a weak core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
such that, for every
\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{c}
, the following equality holds

\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{u})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{u}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*}).

(13)

The core vertex
\mathfrak{c}
induces an exact sequence

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{c}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{s}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}.}

For every
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{c}
, there is another exact sequence

\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{q}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathfrak{c}}}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\frac{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathfrak{q}}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{s}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,\textbf{T}^{*}).}

By the definition of the Fitting ideals, we can deduce that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{c}}\textrm{Fitt}^{i-1}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})_{\mathfrak{q}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

By (
13
), we can deduce that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{c}}\textrm{Fitt}^{i-1}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{q})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

By Proposition
5.11
, we can extend the equality to all the ultraprimes in
\mathcal{P}
:

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}}\textrm{Fitt}^{i-1}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})(\mathfrak{q})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

We can now proceed to prove Theorem
8.25
by induction. For
i=0
, it is Theorem
8.24
for
n=1
. Assuming the result holds true for
i-1
, for all Selmer structures
\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{q})
, we have that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{i-1},\mathfrak{q}\nmid\mathfrak{n}}\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})({\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{n}})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

Rearranging the sum,

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{(\mathcal{F}^{*})({\mathfrak{n}})}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

By Theorem
8.24
, we obtain that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset_{f.i.}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\ind(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})=\mathbf{\Theta}(\kappa).\qed

9
Relation with the classical theory

This section compares the ultra Kolyvagin systems constructed in §
8
with the previous construction in
[
4
]
.

9.1
Ultra Kolyvagin systems with finite coefficients

It is possible to use patched cohomology and ultraprimes to build ultra Kolyvagin systems for a Selmer group with finite coefficient ring. However, no technical advantage appears a result of this construction, but it will be necessary to compare Definition
8.1
with
[
4
, Definition 5.1]

When working with finite coefficients, we are forced to work with exterior biduals instead of exterior powers. The reason is that Selmer groups are no longer necessarily free, so we need to use a rank reduction map analogue to Proposition
6.11
, instead of Corollary
6.12
(see
[
30
, Lemma 2.1]
for a construction of this map for modules over a self-injective ring).

Assumption (SI)
.

Let
R
be a self-injective, local, artinian ring with finite residue field and let
T
be an
R[[G_{K}]]
-module which is free and finitely generated as an
R
-module, ramifies only at finitely many primes, and satisfies Assumption
(BI)
.

The reader is referred to
[
4
, Definition 5.1]
for the classical construction of Kolyvagin systems for Selmer structures defined over
T
. The module of such Kolyvagin systems will be denoted by
\KS(T,\mathcal{F})
. An identical construction will lead to the module of ultra Kolyvagin systems
\textbf{KS}(T,\mathcal{F})
, but considering patched Selmer groups and collection of classes indexed square-free product by Kolyvagin ultraprimes.

The proofs in
[
4
]
also work for studying the module of ultra Kolyvagin systems. In particular, the proof of
[
4
, Theorem 5.2]
implies that
\textbf{KS}(T,\mathcal{F})
is a free
R
-module of rank one whenever
\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure of positive core rank.

The next theorem shows that both constructions lead to the same module.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
R
and
T
be as in Assumption
(SI)
and let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure defined on
T
in the sense of
[
30
, Definition 3.9]
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}}
denote the set of square-free products of Kolyvagin primes, while
\mathcal{N}_{u}
denotes the set of square-free products of Kolyvagin ultraprimes. Then there is an isomorphism

\textbf{KS}(T,\mathcal{F})\to\KS(T,\mathcal{F}):\ \{\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}\}_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{u}}\mapsto\{\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}\}_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}}},

defined by restricting via the inclusion
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{u}
as constant ultraprimes.

The inverse map is is defined by the ultra Kolyvagin system constructed as follows: every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathfrak{u}}
represented by a sequence
(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, the class
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
is constructed by patching the classes
\kappa_{n_{i}}
via the identification

\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\left(\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n_{i})}(K,T)\right).

Remark 9.2
.

Note that, since
R
is finite, there is a canonical inclusion
4
4

4

The inclusion is not an equality since sequences in which the number of prime divisors is not bounded might not represent a (finite) product of Kolyvagin ultraprimes.

\mathcal{N}_{u}\subset\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}})
. Under this identification,
\mathcal{N}_{u}
contains all finite square-free products of constant Kolyvagin ultraprimes, so we can identify
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{u}
.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

It is straightforward that if we have an ultra Kolyvagin system
\kappa=\{\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}\}_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{u}}
in
\textbf{KS}(T_{k},\mathcal{F})
, the subset of
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
when
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}}
is a constant ultraprime forms a classical Kolyvagin system in
\KS(T_{k},\mathcal{F})
.

We can now outline the construction of the inverse map. Assume that
\kappa=\{\kappa_{n}\}_{n\in\mathcal{N}}\in\KS(T_{k},\mathcal{F})
is a classical Kolyvagin system and let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathfrak{u}}
be a square-free product of Kolyvagin ultraprimes. By Remark
4
, we can view
\mathfrak{n}
as an element in
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}})
, so it can be represented by a sequence
(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
n_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}}
. Since the Selmer groups are finite, we can use Corollary
2.9
to conclude that

\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\left(\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n_{i})}(K,T)\right).

Under this identification, the sequence
(\kappa_{n_{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
represents an element in the exterior bidual
\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T_{k})
, that will be defined as
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Note that the construction of
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
is independent of the choice of the representative sequence.

For every ultraprime
\mathfrak{u}=(\ell_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
dividing
\mathfrak{n}
, the sequences
(\phi_{\ell_{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\psi_{\ell_{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
represent, by Proposition
2.11
, elements

\begin{array}[]{cc}\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathfrak{u}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)^{+},&\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{tr}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},T)^{+},\end{array}

respectively, satisfying that
\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}=\phi_{\mathfrak{u}}^{\textrm{fs}}\circ\psi_{\mathfrak{u}}
. We will use those to compute the Kolyvagin system relations.

Since the rank reduction maps commute with the ultraproduct, we have that

\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})=(\alpha_{n_{i},\ell_{i}}(\kappa_{n_{i}}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}},&\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}})=(\beta_{n_{i},\ell_{i}}(\kappa_{n_{i}\ell_{i}}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}},\end{array}

where, again, we are using the following identification:

\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{u}}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T)=\mathcal{U}_{i}\left(\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})-1}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{i}}(n_{i})}(K,T)\right).

Since
\kappa_{n_{i}}
and
\kappa_{n_{i}\ell_{i}}
satisfy the Kolyvagin system relation
\alpha_{n_{i},\ell_{i}}(\kappa_{n_{i}})=\beta_{n_{i},\ell_{i}}(\kappa_{n_{i}\ell_{i}})
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\alpha_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{u}})=(\beta_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{u}}(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{u}})).

Then this construction gives a well defined map

\KS(T,\mathcal{F})\to\textbf{KS}(T,\mathcal{F}).

It is a direct computation to check that the composition

\KS(T,\mathcal{F})\to\textbf{KS}(T,\mathcal{F})\to\KS(T,\mathcal{F})

is the identity map. Indeed, when
\mathfrak{n}
is equivalent to a constant sequence
(n)
, then, by construction, of
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
is the patching of a sequence
(\kappa_{n_{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
n_{i}=n
for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
. Then
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}=\kappa_{n}
.

Since both
\KS(T,\mathcal{F})
and
\textbf{KS}(T,\mathcal{F})
are free
R_{k}
-modules of rank one, the above maps are, in fact, isomorphisms.
∎

9.2
Assumptions

Recall that we established in Notation
5.1
that

\begin{array}[]{cc}\Lambda_{k}:=\Lambda/(p^{k},X^{k}),&\textbf{T}_{k}:=\textbf{T}\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{k}.\end{array}

The goal of this section is to establish conditions on a Selmer structure defined on
T
that ensure that the propagated Selmer structures on
\textbf{T}_{k}
are cartesian in the sense of
[
30
, Definition 3.9]
, so the module of ultra Kolyvagin systems can be studied using Theorem
9.1
.

Assumption (Prop)
.

Assume that
p\geq 5
and let
T
be a Galois representation with coefficients in the Iwasawa algebra satisfying Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Tam)
and let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure defined on
T
such that its propagation to
\textbf{T}/(X)
is also cartesian.

Assumption
(Prop)
can be reinterpreted in terms of the local conditions.

Proposition 9.3
.

Assume that
T
satisfies Assumptions
(BI)
and
(Tam)
. Then Assumption
(Prop)
is equivalent to
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})
being a free Iwasawa module for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that Assumption
(Prop)
holds. In particular,
\mathcal{F}
is cartesian, so
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})
is pseudo-isomorphic to a free module
F
for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
. Since it does not contain any finite submodules, there is a finite module
C
fitting in the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

For the sake of contradiction, assume that
C
is not the trivial module. The snake lemma then induces another exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F/XF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C/XC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

which implies that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)
contains a finite submodule.

However, Proposition
3.6
and diagram chasing induce another exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})[X].}

(14)

When the propagated Selmer structure is cartesian,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T})
contains no finite Selmer modules, so neither does
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)
. Therefore,
C=0
and
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})
is a free Iwasawa module.

Conversely, it is clear that the freeness
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})
for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}}
is stronger than the cartesian condition on
\mathcal{F}
, so we are only left to show that the propagation of
\mathcal{F}
to
\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}
is also cartesian. Remark
5.14
implies that, under Assumption
(Tam)
,
\textbf{H}^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})[X]
contains no finite submodules. If
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})
is a free module, then the snake lemma applied to the short exact sequence in (
14
) implies that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T})
contains no finite submodules either, so the propagated Selmer structure is cartesian on
\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}
.
∎

Assumption
(Prop)
is enough to conclude that the propagated Selmer structures are cartesian on
\textbf{T}_{k}
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
T
and
\mathcal{F}
be as in Assumption
(Prop)
. Then, for all

, the propagation of

to

Proof.

We need to show, for every

, that the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}/\mathfrak{m}\textbf{T})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}_{k})}

\Pi_{1}

Let

By Definition
4.4
, there exists

such that
\Pi_{k}(\gamma)=[X^{k-1}](x)
. The exactness of the last row implies that

, so the injectivity of the rightmost vertical map implies that

. The exactness of the bottom row implies that

so we can find an element

such that

\gamma=X^{k-1}\alpha+X^{k}\beta=X^{k-1}(\alpha+X\beta)\in\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}/p^{k}\textbf{T}).

We claim that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}/p^{k}\textbf{T})
contains no
X
-torsion. Indeed, by diagram chasing, there is an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(p^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Since

By the exactness of the top row,

, so we have that

which concludes the proof of the injectivity of

∎

Next we show that the core rank of
