applied to the short exact sequence in (
16
) and the exactness of the rightmost column follows from Proposition
4.28
. Combining the snake lemma with Proposition
3.27
, we can construct isomorphisms

B\cong\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{n}}\textbf{H}^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})[X]\cong\textbf{H}^{2}(K^{\mathfrak{n}}/K,\textbf{T})[X],

where the last isomorphism holds because
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})=0
. Hence the ranks of
A
and
C
coincide. A double application of the snake lemma shows that

A=\ker\left(I/XI\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{u}\mid\mathfrak{n}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{/\mathcal{F}}(K_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})\otimes\Lambda/(X)\right)=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}[X].

By the structure theorem of finitely generated Iwasawa modules, we have that

\textrm{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}[X]=\textrm{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\otimes\Lambda/(X)\biggr)-\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}.

By Proposition
4.28
and (
15
), we can then conclude that

\begin{aligned} &\chi(\mathcal{F}):=\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})-\textrm{rank}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\\
&\textrm{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T})-\textrm{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*}[X])^{\vee}=\\
&\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}/\mathfrak{m}\textbf{T})-\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*}[\mathfrak{m}]).\end{aligned}\qed

9.3
Comparison theorem

The goal of this section is to generalise the idea in Theorem
9.1
to express the ultra Kolyvagin systems from Definition
8.1
as a limit of classical Kolyvagin systems.

Since, by Proposition
4.6
, an Iwasawa Selmer group can be recovered as the limit

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k}),

where, in the finite Selmer groups,
\mathcal{F}
represents the propagated structure. The first part of the argument will be to generalise this argument to the exterior biduals of Selmer groups.

First, we need to say what are the transition maps used to define the inverse limit. The functoriality of Selmer groups and exterior biduals produces, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, an homomorphism

\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k+1}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k+1})\to\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k+1}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k}).

(17)

The annihilator
\Lambda_{k+1}[(p^{k},X^{k})]
is principal, since it is generated by
pX
. Then there is an isomorphism
\Lambda_{k}\cong\Lambda_{k+1}[(p^{k},X^{k})]
. Then, for every
\Lambda_{k}
-module
M_{k}
,

\Hom(M_{k},\Lambda_{k+1})=\Hom\Bigl(M_{k},\Lambda_{k+1}[(p^{k},X^{k})]\Bigr)=\Hom(M_{k},\Lambda_{k}).

We can then identify

\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k+1}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k})=\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k}).

Then the map in (
17
) can be expressed as

\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k+1}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k+1})\to\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k}).

These maps can be used to construct the inverse limit. In order to compare it with the exterior bidual of the Iwasawa Selmer group, we need to construct the projection maps from the Iwasawa Selmer group.

When Assumption
(BI)
holds,
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})
is a free Iwasawa module by Proposition
5.3
. It is then clear that

\left(\bigcap_{\Lambda}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\right)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{k}=\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{k}\Bigr).

Using the functoriality of Selmer groups and exterior biduals, we can construct a map

\bigcap_{\Lambda}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\to\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k}).

(18)

These maps are compatible when varying
k
, so they induce a map to the inverse limit

\bigcap_{\Lambda}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\to\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcap_{\Lambda_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k}).

(19)

We can show that this map is, in fact, an isomorphism.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a cartesian Selmer structure on
T
such that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
is a free
R
-module. Then the following map is an isomorphism:

\bigcap_{R}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\cong\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\right).

Proof.

Since
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
is a free, finitely generated
R
-module, there is an isomorphism

\bigcap_{R}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\cong\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\otimes_{R}R_{k}\right).

By Proposition
4.26
and the left-exactness of the exterior bidual and the inverse limit, the following map is injective:

\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\otimes_{R}R_{k}\right)\hookrightarrow\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\right).

For the surjectivity, let

(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\right).

Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and every
j\geq k
,

c_{k}\in\textrm{Im}\left(\bigcap_{R_{j}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{j})\to\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\right).

Since the exterior bidual preserves surjections,

c_{k}\in\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\biggl(\textrm{Im}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{j})\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\Bigr)\biggr)\subset\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k}).

Since
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})
by Proposition
4.6
, then

c_{k}\in\bigcap_{R_{k}}^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\biggl(\textrm{Im}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)\to\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})\Bigr)\biggr),

which, using the compactness of
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
, completes the proof of the surjectivity.
∎

Let
\mathcal{N}
be the set of square-free products of Kolyvagin ultraprimes for
T
. Since
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})
is a free, finitely generated Iwasawa module by Proposition
5.3
, there is a canonical identification

\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T})\cong\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,\textbf{T}).

Note that every
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
is a square-free product of Kolyvagin primes for every
\textbf{T}_{k}
. The combination of Theorem
9.1
and Lemma
9.6
allows the construction of a map

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\KS(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F}),

(20)

where the inverse limit is constructed
[
4
, Definition 5.24]
. The above map is, indeed, an isomorphism.

Theorem 9.7
.

Let
T
and
\mathcal{F}
be as in Assumption
(Prop)
. Then there is a canonical identification

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\cong\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

By construction and Lemma
9.6
, the map in (
20
) is injective.

Since both modules are free Iwasawa modules of rank one, in order to show the surjectivity, it is enough to show that the map

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\to\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T}_{1},\mathcal{F})

is surjective. By Proposition
5.11
, there exists a core vertex
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{N}
and, by Proposition
4.28
,

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T}_{1}^{*})=0.

By Theorem
8.7
and
[
4
, Theorem 5.20]
, adapted to consider ultraprimes, the following diagram is commutative with the vertical maps being isomorphisms:

\textstyle{\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T}_{1},\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T}_{1}).}

Since the Selmer groups
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T})
and
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}(\mathfrak{c})}(K,\textbf{T}_{1})
are free
\Lambda
and
\Lambda/\mathfrak{m}
modules of rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})
, respectively, by Proposition
9.5
, then Proposition
5.2
implies that the bottom map is surjective. Hence the top horizontal map

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\twoheadrightarrow\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T}_{1},\mathcal{F})

is surjective as well.
∎

Let
\kappa^{(k)}\in\KS(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F})
. In
[
4
, Definition 5.1]
, the following ideals were constructed:

\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)=\sum_{n\in(\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}})_{i}}\ind(\kappa^{(k)}_{n}),

where
(\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{cl}})_{i}
is the set of square-free products of exactly
i
Kolyvagin primes.

By Theorem
9.1
, we can view
\kappa^{(k)}
as an element in
\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F})
and consider the ideals

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in(\mathcal{N}_{u})_{i}}\ind(\kappa^{(k)}_{\mathfrak{n}})

where, similarly,
(\mathcal{N}_{u})_{i}
denotes the square-free product of exactly
i
Kolyvagin ultraprimes. It will be shown in Corollary
9.10
below that both ideals
\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)
and
\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)
coincide.

For every
\overline{\kappa}=\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)\in\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
, the argument in
[
4
, Definition 5.24]
can be used to construct ideals

\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\overline{\kappa}\Bigr)=\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)\subset\Lambda.

We will show that, under the identification in Theorem
9.7
, the constructions of theta ideals from Kolyvagin systems in
\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
and
\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
are equivalent.

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure satisfying Assumption
(Prop)
, let
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
and let
\overline{\kappa}=\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be its identification under the isomorphism in Theorem
9.7
. Then

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)=\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\overline{\kappa}\Bigr).

The rest of this section is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
9.8
. We start showing the equality between the notion of theta ideals for classical and ultra Kolyvagin systems with finite coefficients.

Lemma 9.9
.

Assume that
\textbf{T}_{k}
and
\mathcal{F}
satisfy Assumption
(SI)
and let
\kappa^{(k)}\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F})
and let
\mathfrak{n}=(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{N}(T_{k})
. For
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
, the following equality holds:

\ind\left(\kappa^{(k)}_{\mathfrak{n}},\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{R_{k}}H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T_{k})\right)=\ind\left(\kappa^{(k)}_{n_{i}},\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{R_{k}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n_{i})}(K,T_{k})\right).

Proof.

Recall from the proof of Theorem
9.1
that

\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T_{k})=\mathcal{U}\left(\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n_{i})}(K,T_{k})\right).

Then
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
can be represented by the sequence
\Bigl(\kappa_{n_{i}}\Bigr)
in this ultraproduct. By Corollary
2.9
, for
\mathcal{U}
-many
i
, there is an isomorphism

\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{n})}(K,T_{k})\cong\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}H^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n_{i})}(K,T_{k})

identifying
\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}}
with
\kappa_{n_{i}}
, which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 9.10
.

Assume that
\textbf{T}_{k}
and
\mathcal{F}
satisfy Assumption
(SI)
and let
\kappa^{(k)}\in\KS(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F})=\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F})
. Then

\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)=\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)

Proof.

It is clear that

\Theta_{i}(\kappa^{(k)})\subset\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa^{(k)})

since the constant Kolyvagin ultraprimes are a subset of the Kolyvagin ultraprimes. Conversely, we need to show that

\ind\biggl(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}},\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}(n)}(K,T)\biggr)\subset\Theta_{i}(\kappa^{(k)}).

However, it follows from Lemma
9.9
, since there exists a square free product of
i
classical Kolyvagin primes
n
such that

\ind(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}})=\ind(\kappa_{n}).\qed

The second part of the proof consists on the study of the preservation of indices of the Kolyvagin classes under the isomorphism in (
19
).

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure such that the Selmer group
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
is a free
R
-module. Let
\alpha\in\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{R}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
and let
\alpha^{(k)}\in\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{R_{k}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T_{k})
be its image under the projection map in (
18
). Then

\ind\left(\alpha,\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\right)+(p^{k},X^{k})=\ind\left(\alpha^{(k)},\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{\Lambda_{k}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k})\right),

where we are identifying the ideals of
\Lambda_{k}
with the ideals of
\Lambda
containing
(p^{k},X^{k})
.

Proof.

Since
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,T)
is a free module, we can consider the class

\alpha+(p^{k},X^{k})\in\left(\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{\Lambda}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\right)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{k}=\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{\Lambda_{k}}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{k}\Bigr).

By construction, it is clear that

\ind(\alpha)+(p^{k},X^{k})=\ind\Bigl(\alpha+(p^{k},X^{k})\Bigr).

Then
\alpha^{(k)}
is the image of
\alpha+(p^{k},X^{k})
under the following map, which is injective by Proposition
4.26
and the left-exactness of the exterior bidual:

\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{\Lambda_{k}}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{k}\Bigr)\hookrightarrow\bigcap^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}_{\Lambda_{k}}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T}_{k}).

Since the domain is a free
\Lambda_{k}
-module, we have that

\ind\Bigl(\alpha^{(k)}\Bigr)=\ind\Bigl(\alpha+(p^{k},X^{k})\Bigr)=\ind(\alpha)+(p^{k},X^{k}).\qed

This result proves an inclusion between theta ideals. Since
\mathcal{N}_{i}(\textbf{T})\subset\mathcal{N}_{i}(\textbf{T}_{k})
, the following corollary holds.

Corollary 9.12
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure defined on
T
satisfying Assumption
(Prop)
and let
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(T,\mathcal{F})
be an ultra Kolyvagin system and let
\kappa^{(k)}\in\textbf{KS}(T_{k},\mathcal{F})
be its projection under the identification in Theorem
9.7
. Then

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)\subset\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa^{(k)}).

We can now conclude the proof of Theorem
9.8
.

Proof of Theorem
9.8
.

By Corollary
9.12
and taking the limit, we can deduce that

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)\subset\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\overline{\kappa}).

Conversely, let

a=(a_{k})\in\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa^{(k)})\subset\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Lambda_{k}=\Lambda.

By Corollary
9.10
, and the construction of theta ideals, there are square-free products
n_{k,1},\ldots,n_{k,s_{k}}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}(\textbf{T}_{k})
of exactly
i
Kolyvagin primes for
\textbf{T}_{k}
such that

a_{k}\in\sum_{i=1}^{s_{k}}\ind\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}_{n_{k,a}}\Bigr).

In an attempt to simplify the notation, we denote
n_{k,i}:=n_{k,s_{k}}
for every
i\geq s_{k}
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathfrak{n}_{a}
be the square-free product of ultraprimes represented by the sequence
(n_{k,a})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. By Lemmas
9.9
and
9.11
, for every fixed
a
and
\mathcal{U}
-many
k
, we have that

\ind(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}})+(p^{k},X^{k})=\ind(\kappa^{(k)}_{n_{k,a}}),

where we are again using the equivalence of ideals of
\Lambda_{k}
and ideals of
\Lambda
containing
(p^{k},X^{k})
. By the compatibility relation of the different Kolyvagin systems in the tower, we have that, for every
j\geq k
,

a_{k}+(p^{k},X^{k})\subset\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\ind(\kappa^{(j)}_{n_{j,a}})+(p^{k},X^{k}).

That implies that

a_{k}+(p^{k},X^{k})\subset\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\ind(\kappa_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}})+(p^{k},X^{k})\subset\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)+(p^{k},X^{k}).

Since
\Lambda
is compact in the profinite topology, every (finitely generated) ideal is also compact, so closed. Since
(p^{k},X^{k})
is a basis of neighbourhoods of the identity and

\Bigl(a+(p^{k},X^{k})\Bigr)\cap\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)\neq\emptyset\ \forall i\in\mathbb{N}.

Then the closure of
\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)
implies that it contains
a
.
∎

We conclude this section with a proof for the question in
[
4
, Remark 5.3]
for the Galois representation
\textbf{T}_{k}
.

Corollary 9.13
.

Suppose that
T
and
\mathcal{F}
satisfy Assumption
(Prop)
and consider a primitive Kolyvagin system
\kappa^{(k)}\in\KS(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F})
. Then

\Theta_{i}(\kappa^{(k)})=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda_{k}}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,(\textbf{T}_{k})^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

Proof.

By Theorems
9.1
and
9.7
, there is a primitive Kolyvagin system
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
that projects to
\kappa^{(k)}
under the map

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\cong\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})=\biggl(\varprojlim_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\KS(\textbf{T}_{j},\mathcal{F})\biggr)\to\KS(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F}).

By Proposition
4.28
, Theorem
8.25
and Corollary
9.12
, we have that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda_{k}}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,(\textbf{T}_{k})^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\otimes\Lambda_{k}\subset\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)\otimes\Lambda_{k}\subset\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr).

The other inclusion is proven in
[
4
, Theorem 5.2]
.
∎

Remark 9.14
.

The argument in Corollary
9.13
is generalisable to other self-injective quotients of
\Lambda
.

9.4
Iwasawa Selmer groups

Theorem
8.25
proved that, when
\mathcal{F}
is a cartesian Selmer structure, the Fitting ideal
\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)
is contained, with finite index, in the ideal
\Theta_{i}(\kappa)
associated with a primitive Kolyvagin system
\kappa\in\KS(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
. The aim of this section is to show that this inclusion is indeed an equality when
\mathcal{F}
satisfies Assumption
(Prop)
.

Theorem 9.15
.

Let
T
be an Iwasawa Galois representation and let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure defined on
T
such that Assumption
(Prop)
holds and
\chi(\mathcal{F})\geq 1
. If
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
is a primitive ultra Kolyvagin system, then

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\ \forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Proof.

Fix
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Theorem
8.25
implies that

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\subset\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa).

Conversely, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\kappa^{(k)}\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F})
the image of
\kappa
under the projection map

\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})\cong\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})=\biggl(\varprojlim_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\KS(\textbf{T}_{j},\mathcal{F})\biggr)\to\KS(\textbf{T}_{k},\mathcal{F}).

By Corollary
9.10
and
[
4
, Theorem 5.2]
,

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)=\Theta_{i}\Bigl(\kappa^{(k)}\Bigr)\subset\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda_{k}}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,(\textbf{T}_{k})^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

By Propositions
4.28
and
6.4
,

\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda_{k}}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,(\textbf{T}_{k})^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)+(p^{k},X^{k}),

where we are identifying ideals of
\Lambda_{k}
with ideals of
\Lambda
that contain
(p^{k},X^{k})
.
Corollary
9.12
implies that

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)+(p^{k},X^{k})\subset\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa^{(k)}).

Taking all of the above into account, we can conclude that

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)+(p^{k},X^{k})\subset\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)+(p^{k},X^{k}).

Since that holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain that

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa)\subset\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).\qed

The following is a generalisation of
[
16
, Proposition 2.9]
, which assumed the vanishing of the Iwasawa
\mu
-invariant associated with the Selmer group.

Theorem 9.16
.

Let
T
be a Galois representation and let
\mathcal{F}
be a Selmer structure satisfying Assumption
(Prop)
such that
\chi(\mathcal{F})\geq 1
. If
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
is a torsion Iwasawa module, then it contains no finite submodules.

Proof.

Let
\kappa\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F})
be a primitive Kolyvagin system. By Theorem
9.15
,

\textrm{Fitt}^{0}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,T^{*})\Bigr)^{\vee}=\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}(\kappa)=\ind(\delta_{1}).

By Proposition
5.3
, then
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})
is a free module of rank
\chi(\mathcal{F})
. Then

\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\cong\Lambda.

Therefore,

\ind\left(\delta_{1},\bigwedge^{\chi(\mathcal{F})}\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(K,\textbf{T})\right)

is a principal ideal, which, by Proposition
6.5
, implies that
\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}
contains no finite submodules.
∎

10
Applications

10.1
Elliptic curves

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be an elliptic curve defined over the rationals. Assume that
T
can be obtained as the tensor product
T_{p}E\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}/\mathbb{Q})]]
, where
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}/\mathbb{Q}
is the cyclotomic
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension.

For this representation, Kato constructed in
[
11
]
an Euler system that, in combination with
[
21
, Theorem 5.3.3]
, produces a Kolyvagin system
\overline{\kappa}_{\textrm{Kato}}\in\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}^{p})
, where
\mathcal{F}^{p}
denotes the Selmer structure relaxed at
p
, in which the local conditions are the finite cohomology groups for finite primes not above
p
and the full local cohomology groups for
p
-adic and archimedean primes (see
[
21
, Definition 5.3.2]
). Note that Proposition
9
implies that
\chi(\mathcal{F}^{p})=1
.

By the isomorphism in Theorem
9.7
, we can see it as an ultra Kolyvagin system
\kappa_{\textrm{Kato}}\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}^{p})
. Then Theorem
8.25
can be used to compute the structure of the fine Selmer group.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be an elliptic curve defined over the rationals, let
\textbf{T}=T_{p}E\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}/\mathbb{Q})]]
and let
\kappa_{\textrm{Kato}}\in\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}^{p})
be the ultra Kolyvagin system obtained from Kato’s Euler system. Assume that:

•

(E1) E has good ordinary reduction at
p\geq 5
.

•

(E2)
G_{K}
acts surjectively on
T_{p}E
.

•

(E3) The Tamagawa numbers of
E
are prime to
p
.

•

(E4)
E(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty,\mathfrak{p}})[p^{\infty}]
is a
p
-divisible group, where
\mathfrak{p}
is the unique prime of
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}
above
p
.

•

(E5) The Iwasawa main conjecture holds for
E/\mathbb{Q}
.

Then

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa_{\textrm{Kato}})=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}(K,\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\ \forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Proof.

The surjectivity assumption implies that
T
satisfies Assumption
(BI)
. In addition, the second and third assumption implies Assumption
(Tam)
(see Remark
5.16
).

Note that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}^{p}}
contains only
p
,
\infty
, and the primes dividing the conductor of
E
. When
\ell\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}
,
[
21
, Lemma 5.3.1]
implies that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\textbf{T})=\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\textbf{T}),

so
\mathcal{F}^{p}
is cartesian. The propagation of
\mathcal{F}
to
\textbf{T}/(X)
is also cartesian by Proposition
9.3
.

Then the result follows from Theorem
9.15
, since the primitivity of Kato’s Kolyvagin system is equivalent to the Iwasawa main conjecture.
∎

Remark 10.2
.

The case in Theorem
10.1
was not covered in
[
26
, Theorem 10.14]
since the Tate module of an elliptic curve is its own Cartier dual.

10.2
Rankin-Selberg convolutions

Let
f
and
g
modular forms of weights
k
and
\ell
, respectively, and levels
N_{f}
and
N_{g}
, respectively. Denote by
\varepsilon_{f}
and
\varepsilon_{g}
the Nebentypus characters of
f
and
g
, respectively. Consider the Galois representation associated with its Rankin-Selberg convolution,

T:=T_{f}\otimes T_{g},

where
T_{f}
and
T_{g}
are the Galois representations associated with
f
and
g
. As usual, denote

\textbf{T}:=T\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}/\mathbb{Q})]].

In
[
17
]
, Kato’s construction was generalised by Lei, Loeffler and Zerbes to build an Euler system for the representation
T
, that will be denoted by
\textbf{z}_{\textrm{LLZ}}
.

Remark 10.3
.

In this case, we cannot obtain a Kolyvagin system in
\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},\mathcal{F}^{p})
using
[
21
, Theorem 5.3.3]
and Theorem
9.7
since
\chi(\mathcal{F}^{p})=2
. Note that the fact
\chi(\mathcal{F}^{p})=2
can be seen from Proposition
9
, since
d_{-}(T)=2
because both
T_{f}
and
T_{g}
are odd representations and all the other terms in the formula vanish. It is important to remark that, since

H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\textbf{T}^{*})=H^{0}((\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})_{\infty},T^{*}),

is a torsion Iwasawa module for every finite prime
\ell
, where
(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})_{\infty}
is the cyclotomic
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
, only
p
and
\infty
contribute to the formula in (
9
).

However, it is possible, under the following assumption, to use
\textbf{z}_{\textrm{LLZ}}
to obtain a Kolyvagin system for a different Selmer structure. In order to do that, we need to make the following assumption on
f
and
g
.

Assumption (RS)
.

Assume that

•

(RS1) Both
f
and
g
are ordinary at
p\geq 5
. In addition, assume the weights of
f
and
g
are at least
2
.

•

(RS2) The action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
on
T_{f}\otimes T_{g}
satisfies Assumption
(BI)
.

•

(RS3) The cohomology groups
H^{0}((K_{\infty})_{\ell},\textbf{T}^{*})
are
p
-divisible when
\ell
is either the prime
p
or a prime divisor of
N_{f}N_{g}
.

Remark 10.4
.

By
[
20
, Proposition 4.3.1]
, Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
will never hold if
\epsilon_{f}\epsilon_{g}=1
. However, it is shown in
[
12
]
that Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
holds for all but finitely many primes in the case where
k,\ell\geq 2
, at least one of them is odd,
(N_{f},N_{g})=1
and neither
f
nor
g
is of CM-type. The reader is referred to
[
20
]
and
[
31
]
for more involved situations in which Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
is guaranteed to hold.

Since
f
(resp.
g
) is ordinary,
T_{f}
(resp.
T_{g}
) contains a subrepresentation
T_{f}^{+}
(resp.
T_{g}^{+}
) of weight
k-1
(resp.
\ell-1
). Then the quotients
T_{f}/T_{f}^{+}
and
T_{g}/T_{g}^{+}
have Hodge-Tate weight
0
.

Under this assumption, we can consider the Panchiskin subrepresentation

T^{+}:=T_{f}^{+}\otimes T_{g}+T_{f}\otimes T_{g}^{+}.

Note that
T^{+}
has Hodge-Tate weights
\{k-1,\ell-1,k+\ell-2\}
while
T/T^{+}
has Hodge-Tate
0
. We can consider the Iwasawa analogue

\textbf{T}^{+}:=T^{+}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}/\mathbb{Q})]]\subset\textbf{T}.

Moreover, denote the quotients

\begin{array}[]{cc}\textbf{T}^{-}:=\textbf{T}/\textbf{T}^{+},&T^{-}:=T/T^{+},\end{array}

which are free Iwasawa and p-adic modules, respectively, of rank one.

We can define the following Selmer structure of Greenberg-type, which was first defined in
[
8
]
.

Definition 10.5
.

The Greenberg Selmer structure
{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Gr}}}
is defined by the local conditions
5
5

5

There is no need to define a local condition at the archimedean prime, since we are assuming that
p
is odd.

\textbf{H}^{1}_{{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Gr}}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\textbf{H}^{1}_{{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Gr}}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathfrak{u}},\textbf{T}),\ \textrm{if $\mathfrak{u}\neq p,$}\\
&\textbf{H}^{1}_{{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Gr}}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})=\ker\biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})\biggr).\end{aligned}\right.

In order to compute the local condition at
p
, we make the following further assumptions.

Assumption (RS)
.

Assume that

•

(RS4)
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-}\otimes\Lambda/\mathfrak{m})=0
,

•

(RS5)
T^{+}
does not admit any non-zero quotient in which
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
acts by the cyclotomic character.

Remark 10.6
.

Under Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
, the Weil bound implies that Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
will hold provided that at least one of the weights is at least
3
.

Proposition 10.7
.

Under Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
,

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Gr}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})=\textrm{Im}\biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{+})\to\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})\biggr).

Proof.

Since

\textrm{Im}\biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{+})\to\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})\biggr)=\ker\biggl(\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})\to\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})\biggr),

we are only left to show that the map

\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})\to\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})

is injective. By the inflation-restriction sequence, that is equivalent to show that

\textbf{H}^{1}\Bigl(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{H}^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})\Bigr)=0,

where
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}}
denotes the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. If
\textbf{T}^{-}
is ramified, then it necessarily holds that
\textbf{H}^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})=0
. Otherwise, we have that
\textbf{H}^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})=\textbf{T}^{-}
and, by Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
,

\textbf{H}^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})=0.

In the second case, note that there is an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{T}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma-1}
\textstyle{\textbf{T}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
\gamma
denotes the Frobenius element in
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. By the additivity of the rank,
\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})
is a torsion
\Lambda
-module. However, Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
is stronger than
\textbf{H}^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}},\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-}/f\textbf{T}^{-})=0
, where
f
is any element in
\Lambda
. Then we can conclude that

\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\textrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})_{\textrm{tors}}=0,

so this cohomology group necessarily vanishes.
∎

With the identification in Proposition
10.7
, it is shown within the proof of
[
18
, Corollary B.1.5]
that the Greenberg local condition coincides with the Bloch-Kato condition.

Proposition 10.8
.

(
[
18
, Corollary B.1.5]
)
If we assume
•
‣
(RS)
,
•
‣
(RS)
and
•
‣
(RS)
, then

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})=\textbf{H}^{1}_{{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Gr}}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}).

By
[
17
, Proposition 6.5.4]
, the Euler system
\textbf{z}_{\textrm{LLZ}}
of Lei, Loeffler and Zerbes satisfies the following local conditions: for every number abelian extension
F/\mathbb{Q}
and every prime
w
of
F
,

\textrm{loc}_{w}((\textbf{z}_{\textrm{LLZ}})_{F})\in H^{1}_{\textrm{f}}(F_{w},T).

By
[
18
, Corollary B.1.5]
and
[
12
, Proposition 12.2.3]
, the Kolyvagin derivative produces a Kolyvagin system

\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}}\in\overline{\KS}(\textbf{T},{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}})=\textbf{KS}(\textbf{T},{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}}),

where
{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}}
represents the Bloch-Kato Selmer structure
6
6

6

This descent process is finer than the one described in
[
21
]
, since the Kolyvagin system obtained is associated with the Bloch-Kato Selmer structure instead of the canonical one.
and we are using the identification in Theorem
9.7
.

The following corollary is obtained as an application of Theorem
8.25
.

Theorem 10.9
.

Under Assumptions
•
‣
(RS)
-
•
‣
(RS)
, there exists
\delta\in\Lambda
, measuring the divisibility
\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}}
, defined independently of
i
such that

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}})=\delta\cdot\textrm{Fitt}^{i}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{({\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}})^{*}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\infty},\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr)\ \forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Moreover,
\delta
can be taken equal to
1
when the Iwasawa main conjecture holds.

Remark 10.10
.

The Iwasawa main conjecture for the Rankin-Selberg convolution has been proven in some cases in
[
34
]
.

Proof of Theorem
10.9
.

Note that Assumption
(BI)
is guaranteed by
•
‣
(RS)
and Assumption
(Tam)
, by
•
‣
(RS)
.
[
21
, Lemma 5.3.1 (ii)]
implies, when
\ell\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{F}^{*}}
, that

\textbf{H}^{1}_{\mathcal{F}^{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\textbf{T})=\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\textbf{T}),

so these local conditions are cartesian. By Proposition
10.8
,

\textbf{H}^{1}_{/{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-}).

(21)

As shown in the proof of Proposition
10.7
, Assumption
•
‣
(RS)
implies that the latter is torsion-free, which implies that the Bloch-Kato condition is cartesian.

Moreover, we can see that the map in (
21
) is an isomorphism. Note that its cokernel is isomorphic to
\textbf{H}^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{+})[X]
. By local duality, there is an isomorphism

\textbf{H}^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{+})\cong\textbf{H}^{0}\Bigl(\mathbb{Q}_{p},(\textbf{T}^{+})^{*}\Bigr)=\textbf{H}^{0}\Bigl((\mathbb{Q}_{\infty})_{\mathfrak{p}},(T^{+})^{*}\Bigr)=0,

where
\mathfrak{p}
is the unique prime of
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}
above
p
and the last equality follows from assumption
•
‣
(RS)
and the fact that
\Gal((\mathbb{Q}_{\infty})_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a pro-
p
group. Hence,

\textbf{H}^{1}_{/{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T})\cong\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-}).

The same argument used to show the torsion-freeness of
\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})
leads to

\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-}/X\textbf{T}^{-})[p]=0.

By
•
‣
(RS)
, there is an injection

\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})\otimes\Lambda/(X)\hookrightarrow\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-}/X\textbf{T}^{-}),

so we can conclude that the first is a free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module. This, together with the absence torsion, is enough to conclude that
\textbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\textbf{T}^{-})
is a free Iwasawa module (see the argument in Proposition
5.3
). Then Proposition
9.3
shows that the propagation of
{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}}
to
\textbf{T}/X\textbf{T}
is cartesian.

The Kolyvagin system
\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}}
might not be primitive, but there exist a primitive one
\kappa\in\KS(\textbf{T},{\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}})
and
\delta\in\Lambda
such that
\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}}=\delta\cdot\kappa
. Then Theorem
9.15
implies that

\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}(\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}})=\delta\Theta_{i}(\kappa)=\textrm{Fitt}^{i}\Bigl(\textbf{H}^{1}_{({\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{BK}}})^{*}}(\mathbb{Q},\textbf{T}^{*})^{\vee}\Bigr).

The Iwasawa main conjecture is equivalent to the primitivity of
\kappa_{\textrm{LLZ}}
, meaning that
\delta\in\Lambda^{\times}
.
∎
